An MLM-only variant (without classification) produces the same pattern (AA Full = 0.874$\pm$0.014); representation drift from continued pretraining, not classification-specific bias, is the underlying cause. Domain-adaptive fine-tuning narrows the representation space of a sufficiently large encoder and reduces its inherent OOD generalization ability.
333	
334	\section{TOST Equivalence Sensitivity Analysis}
335	
336	\begin{table}[ht]
337	\centering
338	\footnotesize
339	\caption{TOST equivalence test sensitivity to margin $\delta$ (scrambled vs.\ 9-class). Rows grouped by seed count: $n$=3 (42, 123, 456), $n$=5 (+789, 1000), $n$=10 (+2024, 2025, 2026, 314, 628). $p$-values from two one-sided $t$-tests; $p < 0.05$ confirms equivalence within $\pm\delta$.}
340	\label{tab:tost_sensitivity}
341	\begin{tabular}{@{}llccc@{}}
342	\toprule
343	\textbf{Dataset} & $n$ & $\delta$=5\,pp & $\delta$=3\,pp & $\delta$=2\,pp \\
344	\midrule
345	DD OOD & 3 & .006 \checkmark & .016 \checkmark & .035 \checkmark \\
346	DD OOD & 5 & ${<}.001$ \checkmark & ${<}.001$ \checkmark & .002 \checkmark \\
347	DD OOD & 10 & ${<}10^{-8}$ \checkmark & ${<}10^{-8}$ \checkmark & ${<}10^{-8}$ \checkmark \\
348	\midrule
349	FITD OOD & 10 & ${<}10^{-8}$ \checkmark & ${<}10^{-8}$ \checkmark & ${<}10^{-8}$ \checkmark \\
350	\midrule
351	ActorAttack & 3 & .290 & .428 & .507 \\
352	ActorAttack & 5 & .080 & .200 & .310 \\
353	ActorAttack & 10 & .183 & --- & --- \\
354	\bottomrule
355	\end{tabular}
356	\end{table}
357	
358	On DD, TOST confirms scrambled $\approx$ correct labels even at $\delta$=2\,pp ($p$=0.035 at $n$=3), demonstrating that label correctness is not a detection factor; the DD equivalence conclusion is robust to both margin choice and seed count ($p < 10^{-8}$ at $n$=10). On FITD, equivalence is similarly confirmed ($p < 10^{-8}$, $d \approx 0$ at $n$=10). On ActorAttack, extending from $n$=5 to $n$=10 reveals a moderate effect: Cohen's $d$ increases from 0.09 to 0.44 (medium), with 9-class outperforming scrambled by 3\,pp (0.979 vs.\ 0.949). TOST remains inconclusive ($p$\,=\,0.183 at $\delta$=5\,pp), and variance is driven by seed 314 (scrambled F1\,=\,0.833). Label correctness is thus attack-complexity-dependent: negligible on template-based families (DD, FITD) but moderate on structurally complex attacks (ActorAttack).
359	
360	\section{Reproducibility Details}
361	\label{sec:reproducibility}
362	
363	\paragraph{Stage-1 training.}
364	All variants use DeBERTa-v3-base (184M parameters) with learning rate $2 \times 10^{-5}$, AdamW optimizer ($\beta_1$=0.9, $\beta_2$=0.999, weight decay 0.01), linear warmup over the first 10\% of steps, batch size 16, and 4 epochs. MLM variants use 15\% random token masking. Training takes approximately 30 minutes per variant on a single NVIDIA A100 (40GB).
365	
366	\paragraph{Stage-2 training.}
367	The BiGRU has hidden dimension 256, 2 layers, and dropout 0.3 (bidirectional, so output dimension 512). The MLP classifier has layers $512 \to 256 \to 2$ with ReLU activation. Training uses learning rate $1 \times 10^{-3}$, Adam optimizer, batch size 32, and 20 epochs with early stopping (patience 5) on validation F1. Stage-2 training completes in under 5 minutes per variant.
368	
369	\paragraph{Data generation.}
370	Jailbreak conversations are generated using Qwen3-8B with attack-specific prompt templates. Crescendo conversations follow a 5--8 turn gradual escalation structure. FITD conversations use a 2--3 turn commitment-consistency structure. Deceptive Delight conversations embed harmful content within emotionally engaging 3--5 turn sequences. Benign conversations are generated with topic-anchored prompts across five domains (technology, health, finance, education, general) with matched turn counts.
371	
372	\paragraph{Strategy annotation.}
373	Each user turn is annotated by Qwen3-8B using a structured prompt that provides definitions and two examples for each of the nine strategy categories. The prompt instructs the model to output a single category label. We plan to release all generation and annotation prompts, the full dataset, and training code upon acceptance.
374	
375	\paragraph{Adversarial paraphrasing.}
376	The paraphrased test set is generated by instructing Qwen3-8B to rephrase each user turn while preserving the communicative intent. The resulting paraphrases have mean BERTScore 0.92 and mean lexical overlap 0.20 with the originals. These metrics confirm substantial surface-level variation.
377	
378	\paragraph{Cross-checkpoint variance.}
379	To disentangle Stage-1 from Stage-2 variance, we evaluate three independently trained 9-class DAPT checkpoints (seeds 42, 123, 456) with a fixed downstream BiGRU seed (42). Results: IID F1\,=\,1.000$\pm$0.000, DD OOD\,=\,0.926$\pm$0.026, AA OOD\,=\,0.935$\pm$0.013. Stage-1 training is stable (OOD std\,$\leq$\,2.6\,pp), confirming that the variance reported in the main paper is not dominated by Stage-1 initialization.
380	
381	\section{End-to-End Fine-Tuning Upper Bound}
382	\label{app:e2e}
383	
384	\begin{table}[ht]
385	\centering

      "content": "Crescendo conversations exhibit broad transition coverage across all nine strategy categories (1,818 total transitions), with the highest concentration in Role Assignment self-transitions (545); this reflects its gradual escalation design.\nFITD conversations show a markedly sparser pattern (541 transitions), with Role$\\to$Obfuscation (161) as the dominant transition, consistent with FITD's commitment-consistency exploitation~\\citep{cialdini2001influence}.\nDD conversations span 5 active source strategies (565 transitions), with Emotion$\\to$Obfuscation (129) as the dominant transition, aligning with DD's design of embedding harmful content within emotionally engaging contexts~\\citep{deceptivedelight2024}.\n\n\\section{Centered CKA Heatmaps}\n\\label{sec:cka_heatmap}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/paper/cka_heatmap.pdf}\n\\caption{Centered CKA similarity~\\citep{kornblith2019cka} between encoder variants on IID and OOD data for DeBERTa-v3 and RoBERTa. Low off-diagonal values (vanilla vs.\\ adapted variants) confirm that continued pretraining substantially reshapes representations; the MLM vs.\\ 9-class block shows distinct geometries (CKA $\\leq$ 0.2 on RoBERTa OOD).}\n\\label{fig:cka_heatmap}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\section{Embedding Space Visualization}\n\\label{app:tsne}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:tsne} visualizes mean-pooled \\texttt{[CLS]} embeddings via t-SNE for three representative variants (Vanilla, JB-MLM, 9-Class) on DD OOD and ActorAttack. Vanilla embeddings show dispersed jailbreak clusters with ambiguous decision boundaries, while domain-adapted variants (JB-MLM, 9-Class) produce tighter, more separable clusters. JB-MLM achieves the clearest bimodal separation on both evaluation sets, consistent with its superior detection performance.\n\n\\begin{figure}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/paper/embedding_tsne.pdf}\n\\caption{t-SNE visualization of mean-pooled \\texttt{[CLS]} embeddings (perplexity=30) for Vanilla, JB-MLM, and 9-Class variants on DD OOD and ActorAttack (118 samples each; blue=benign, red=jailbreak).}\n\\label{fig:tsne}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\section{Cross-LLM Validation}\n\\label{app:cross_llm}\n\n\\begin{table}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\footnotesize\n\\caption{Cross-LLM validation (F1, full conversation). All models are trained on Qwen3-8B data; conditions vary the source of jailbreak (JB) and benign (ben.) conversations at inference. Values are evaluated under a matched 50-sample subset protocol and differ from Table~\\ref{tab:evidence_hierarchy}, which uses the full evaluation set.}\n\\label{tab:cross_llm}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccccc@{}}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{3}{c}{\\textit{DD Full}} & \\multicolumn{3}{c}{\\textit{AA Full}} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-4} \\cmidrule(lr){5-7}\n\\textbf{Condition} & Van. & MLM & 9-cl. & Van. & MLM & 9-cl. \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen JB + Qwen ben. & .312 & .914 & .997 & .258 & .893 & .972 \\\\\nLlama JB + Llama ben. & .261 & .282 & .433 & .261 & .368 & .321 \\\\\nLlama JB + Qwen ben. & .302 & .778$^{*}$ & 1.000 & .302 & .903 & .801 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\vspace{-2pt}\n{\\footnotesize $^{*}$High seed variance (std = 0.306): seed 456 outlier (F1 = 0.345); remaining two seeds achieve F1 $\\geq$ 0.97.}\n\\end{table}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:cross_llm} shows three inference conditions. When both jailbreak and benign text come from Llama (row~2), all variants collapse (F1 = 0.26--0.43), but this conflates two sources of distribution shift: unfamiliar jailbreak patterns and unfamiliar benign text. Row~3 isolates jailbreak transferability by pairing Llama jailbreaks with the original Qwen benign distribution. Under this condition, 9-class recovers to F1 = 1.000 on DD and 0.801 on ActorAttack, and JB-MLM reaches 0.903 on ActorAttack. The contrast between rows~2 and~3 shows that the dominant failure mode in row~2 is benign distribution mismatch (false positives from unfamiliar benign text), not inability to recognize cross-LLM jailbreak patterns. Vanilla remains at 0.26--0.31 across all conditions, further confirming that encoders without DAPT lack OOD detection capability regardless of the LLM source. The residual degradation on ActorAttack (0.801 vs.\\ 0.972) reflects genuine cross-LLM variation in structurally complex attack patterns.\n\n\\begin{table}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\footnotesize\n\\caption{Error decomposition for DD Full cross-LLM conditions (matched 50-sample subsets). TP rate is identical across benign conditions; the F1 collapse in the Llama-only condition (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_llm}, row~2) is driven entirely by false positives on unfamiliar benign text.}\n\\label{tab:cross_llm_error}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Metric} & Van. & MLM & 9-cl. \\\\\n\\midrule\nTP rate (Llama JB) & 0.0\\% & 68.0\\% & 100.0\\% \\\\\nFP rate (Llama ben.) & 29.3\\% & 88.0\\% & 90.0\\% \\\\\nFP rate (Qwen ben.) & 0.0\\% & 0.9\\% & 0.0\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:cross_llm_error} decomposes DD Full F1 into true positive and false positive rates. TP rates are identical regardless of the benign source: domain-adapted models detect Llama-generated jailbreaks at the same rate whether evaluated against Qwen or Llama benign text. The F1 collapse in the full Llama condition (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_llm}, row~2) is driven entirely by false positives on unfamiliar benign text (FP rate: 88--90\\% on Llama vs.\\ 0--0.9\\% on Qwen for adapted variants). The model implicitly treats non-Qwen text style as a jailbreak indicator, a spurious correlation from single-LLM training data. This failure reflects a data diversity limitation, not a methodological one; mixed-LLM training (Table~\\ref{tab:mixed_llm}) partially addresses this confound.\n\n\\section{Mixed-LLM Training Results}\n\\label{app:mixed_llm}\n\n\\begin{table}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\footnotesize\n\\caption{Mixed-LLM training (250 Qwen + 250 Llama conversations, 3-seed mean$\\pm$std where available). ToxicChat evaluated on balanced set (408 conversations).}\n\\label{tab:mixed_llm}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lccccc@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Variant} & Mixed & DD OOD & AA OOD & FITD OOD & ToxicChat \\\\\n\\midrule\n9-class & \\textbf{.996$\\pm$.006} & \\textbf{.818$\\pm$.096} & \\textbf{.504$\\pm$.107} & \\textbf{.997$\\pm$.005} & \\textbf{.550$\\pm$.052} \\\\\nMLM & .681$\\pm$.226 & .289$\\pm$.101 & .258$\\pm$.075 & .679$\\pm$.188 & .256$\\pm$.053 \\\\\nVanilla & .802$\\pm$.010 & .291$\\pm$.065 & .389$\\pm$.073 & .867$\\pm$.022 & .356$\\pm$.009 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:mixed_llm} reports detection performance when training on a mixed corpus of Qwen3-8B and Llama-3.1-8B conversations. Classification-based DAPT (9-class) maintains the strongest DD OOD performance (0.818$\\pm$0.096), outperforming both vanilla (0.291$\\pm$0.065) and MLM (0.289$\\pm$0.101) by 53\\,pp. ActorAttack remains challenging under mixed-LLM conditions (9-class F1\\,=\\,0.504$\\pm$0.107), suggesting structurally complex attacks require larger or more diverse training data. ToxicChat performance shows no significant improvement over single-source training (0.550$\\pm$0.052 vs.\\ 0.596$\\pm$0.087; Section~\\ref{sec:human_data}), consistent with ToxicChat being zero-shot single-turn transfer rather than a standard OOD benchmark.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-LLM training pilot.}\nTo test whether mixed-source training preserves Qwen OOD detection while enabling cross-LLM transfer, we train 9-class DAPT on a combined corpus: 175 Qwen jailbreak + 175 Qwen benign + 164 Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct jailbreak + 150 WildChat benign ($n$\\,=\\,664 total; 3-seed mean). Qwen OOD detection is fully preserved (DD/AA/FITD F1\\,=\\,1.000 across all families), and WildChat FPR remains 0\\%. On held-out Llama-generated conversations, Crescendo recall reaches 100\\% ($n$\\,=\\,40; 95\\% CI lower bound 91.2\\%). Llama DD, AA, and FITD each achieve 100\\% recall, but with only $n$\\,=\\,10 per family the 95\\% CI lower bound is 72.2\\%, limiting the strength of this evidence. These results are preliminary: they confirm that mixed-LLM training does not degrade existing detection and provide initial evidence of cross-LLM transfer, but definitive conclusions require larger Llama-generated test sets.\n\n\\section{Dataset Scale Validation}\n\\label{app:scale_validation}\n\n\\begin{table}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\footnotesize\n\\caption{Dataset composition validation (1{,}000 conversations from a different generation run with different topic seeds and attack composition than the 500-conversation training set; no sample overlap). IID results omitted due to train/test overlap (71\\% of original test set present in expanded data). Note: the expanded corpus uses different benign sources (LLM-synthetic vs.\\ WildChat-anchored) and excludes cipher-based attack variants present in the original; performance differences may reflect composition changes rather than scale effects.}\n\\label{tab:scale_validation}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Variant} & DD OOD & AA OOD \\\\\n\\midrule\n9-class & \\textbf{.889$\\pm$.041} & \\textbf{.913$\\pm$.041} \\\\\nMLM & .338$\\pm$.010 & .351$\\pm$.018 \\\\\nVanilla & .326$\\pm$.000 & .508$\\pm$.085 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:scale_validation} compares the original 500-conversation training set with a separately generated 1{,}000-conversation corpus. The hierarchy 9-class $\\gg$ MLM $\\approx$ vanilla is preserved, but the 5--10\\,pp OOD degradation relative to the original (9-class DD: 0.889 vs.\\ 0.994) likely reflects corpus composition differences---the expanded set uses different benign generation sources and excludes cipher-based attack variants---rather than a scale effect. This underscores that training data composition, not volume, is the primary determinant of detection quality.\n\n\\section{RoBERTa-large DAPT Learning Rate Control}\n\n\\begin{table}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\footnotesize\n\\caption{RoBERTa-large (355M) DAPT learning rate control on OOD Full (3-seed mean$\\pm$std). Reducing the learning rate worsens both DD and AA, ruling out over-adaptation as the cause of the regression in Table~\\ref{tab:cross_encoder}.}\n\\label{tab:roberta_large_lr}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Config} & DD Full & AA Full \\\\\n\\midrule\nVanilla (no DAPT) & .927$\\pm$.036 & 1.000$\\pm$.000 \\\\\n9-class, LR=$2 \\times 10^{-5}$ & .987$\\pm$.004 & .946$\\pm$.057 \\\\\n9-class, LR=$1 \\times 10^{-5}$ & .948$\\pm$.034 & .781$\\pm$.054 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nReducing the DAPT learning rate from $2 \\times 10^{-5}$ to $1 \\times 10^{-5}$ worsens both DD and AA performance, which rules out over-adaptation as the cause of the regression observed in Table~\\ref{tab:cross_encoder}. An MLM-only variant (without classification) produces the same pattern (AA Full = 0.874$\\pm$0.014); representation drift from continued pretraining, not classification-specific bias, is the underlying cause. Domain-adaptive fine-tuning narrows the representation space of a sufficiently large encoder and reduces its inherent OOD generalization ability.\n\n\\section{TOST Equivalence Sensitivity Analysis}\n\n\\begin{table}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\footnotesize\n\\caption{TOST equivalence test sensitivity to margin $\\delta$ (scrambled vs.\\ 9-class). Rows grouped by seed count: $n$=3 (42, 123, 456), $n$=5 (+789, 1000), $n$=10 (+2024, 2025, 2026, 314, 628). $p$-values from two one-sided $t$-tests; $p < 0.05$ confirms equivalence within $\\pm\\delta$.}\n\\label{tab:tost_sensitivity}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llccc@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Dataset} & $n$ & $\\delta$=5\\,pp & $\\delta$=3\\,pp & $\\delta$=2\\,pp \\\\\n\\midrule\nDD OOD & 3 & .006 \\checkmark & .016 \\checkmark & .035 \\checkmark \\\\\nDD OOD & 5 & ${<}.001$ \\checkmark & ${<}.001$ \\checkmark & .002 \\checkmark \\\\\nDD OOD & 10 & ${<}10^{-8}$ \\checkmark & ${<}10^{-8}$ \\checkmark & ${<}10^{-8}$ \\checkmark \\\\\n\\midrule\nFITD OOD & 10 & ${<}10^{-8}$ \\checkmark & ${<}10^{-8}$ \\checkmark & ${<}10^{-8}$ \\checkmark \\\\\n\\midrule\nActorAttack & 3 & .290 & .428 & .507 \\\\\nActorAttack & 5 & .080 & .200 & .310 \\\\\nActorAttack & 10 & .183 & --- & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nOn DD, TOST confirms scrambled $\\approx$ correct labels even at $\\delta$=2\\,pp ($p$=0.035 at $n$=3), demonstrating that label correctness is not a detection factor; the DD equivalence conclusion is robust to both margin choice and seed count ($p < 10^{-8}$ at $n$=10). On FITD, equivalence is similarly confirmed ($p < 10^{-8}$, $d \\approx 0$ at $n$=10). On ActorAttack, extending from $n$=5 to $n$=10 reveals a moderate effect: Cohen's $d$ increases from 0.09 to 0.44 (medium), with 9-class outperforming scrambled by 3\\,pp (0.979 vs.\\ 0.949). TOST remains inconclusive ($p$\\,=\\,0.183 at $\\delta$=5\\,pp), and variance is driven by seed 314 (scrambled F1\\,=\\,0.833). Label correctness is thus attack-complexity-dependent: negligible on template-based families (DD, FITD) but moderate on structurally complex attacks (ActorAttack).\n\n\\section{Reproducibility Details}\n\\label{sec:reproducibility}\n\n\\paragraph{Stage-1 training.}\nAll variants use DeBERTa-v3-base (184M parameters) with learning rate $2 \\times 10^{-5}$, AdamW optimizer ($\\beta_1$=0.9, $\\beta_2$=0.999, weight decay 0.01), linear warmup over the first 10\\% of steps, batch size 16, and 4 epochs. MLM variants use 15\\% random token masking. Training takes approximately 30 minutes per variant on a single NVIDIA A100 (40GB).\n\n\\paragraph{Stage-2 training.}\nThe BiGRU has hidden dimension 256, 2 layers, and dropout 0.3 (bidirectional, so output dimension 512). The MLP classifier has layers $512 \\to 256 \\to 2$ with ReLU activation. Training uses learning rate $1 \\times 10^{-3}$, Adam optimizer, batch size 32, and 20 epochs with early stopping (patience 5) on validation F1. Stage-2 training completes in under 5 minutes per variant.\n\n\\paragraph{Data generation.}\nJailbreak conversations are generated using Qwen3-8B with attack-specific prompt templates. Crescendo conversations follow a 5--8 turn gradual escalation structure. FITD conversations use a 2--3 turn commitment-consistency structure. Deceptive Delight conversations embed harmful content within emotionally engaging 3--5 turn sequences. Benign conversations are generated with topic-anchored prompts across five domains (technology, health, finance, education, general) with matched turn counts.\n\n\\paragraph{Strategy annotation.}\nEach user turn is annotated by Qwen3-8B using a structured prompt that provides definitions and two examples for each of the nine strategy categories. The prompt instructs the model to output a single category label. We plan to release all generation and annotation prompts, the full dataset, and training code upon acceptance.\n\n\\paragraph{Adversarial paraphrasing.}\nThe paraphrased test set is generated by instructing Qwen3-8B to rephrase each user turn while preserving the communicative intent. The resulting paraphrases have mean BERTScore 0.92 and mean lexical overlap 0.20 with the originals. These metrics confirm substantial surface-level variation.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-checkpoint variance.}\nTo disentangle Stage-1 from Stage-2 variance, we evaluate three independently trained 9-class DAPT checkpoints (seeds 42, 123, 456) with a fixed downstream BiGRU seed (42). Results: IID F1\\,=\\,1.000$\\pm$0.000, DD OOD\\,=\\,0.926$\\pm$0.026, AA OOD\\,=\\,0.935$\\pm$0.013. Stage-1 training is stable (OOD std\\,$\\leq$\\,2.6\\,pp), confirming that the variance reported in the main paper is not dominated by Stage-1 initialization.\n\n\\section{End-to-End Fine-Tuning Upper Bound}\n\\label{app:e2e}\n\n\\begin{table}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\footnotesize\n\\caption{Frozen probing vs.\\ end-to-end (E2E) fine-tuning for DeBERTa-v3-base 9-class (3-seed mean$\\pm$std). E2E unfreezes the Stage-1 encoder during Stage-2 training. Frozen values use main-experiment checkpoints (matching Tables~\\ref{tab:main_results}--\\ref{tab:evidence_hierarchy}); Table~\\ref{tab:frozen_verify} uses plan\\_002 checkpoints, yielding higher frozen accuracy (DD\\,=\\,.997, AA\\,=\\,.972) and a narrower E2E gap ($\\leq$2.5\\,pp).}\n\\label{tab:e2e}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Config} & IID & DD OOD & AA OOD \\\\\n\\midrule\nFrozen probing & 1.000$\\pm$.000 & .994$\\pm$.005 & .939$\\pm$.047 \\\\\nE2E fine-tuning & 1.000$\\pm$.000 & 1.000$\\pm$.000 & \\textbf{.997$\\pm$.005} \\\\\n\\midrule\n$\\Delta$ & .000 & +.006 & +.058 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}",

{"result":"{\"success\": true, \"data\": {\"stdout\": \"\\\"\\\"\\\"\\nScrambled-label ablation for DisPeD.\\nRandomly permutes persuasion_strategy labels within jailbreak conversations,\\npreserving the label distribution but breaking turn-strategy correspondence.\\n\\nUsage:\\n    python src/scramble_labels.py --input data/final_v2/train.jsonl --output data/final_v2/train_scrambled.jsonl --seed 42\\n\\\"\\\"\\\"\\nimport json, argparse, random\\n\\n\\ndef scramble_strategies(conversations, seed=42):\\n    rng = random.Random(seed)\\n    result = []\\n\\n    for conv in conversations:\\n        conv_copy = json.loads(json.dumps(conv))\\n\\n        if conv_copy.get('label') == 'jailbreak':\\n            strategies = []\\n            for turn in conv_copy['turns']:\\n                if turn.get('role') == 'user':\\n                    s = turn.get('persuasion_label', turn.get('persuasion_strategy', 0))\\n                    strategies.append(s)\\n\\n            rng.shuffle(strategies)\\n\\n            idx = 0\\n            for turn in conv_copy['turns']:\\n                if turn.get('role') == 'user':\\n                    if 'persuasion_label' in turn:\\n                        turn['persuasion_label'] = strategies[idx]\\n                    if 'persuasion_strategy' in turn:\\n                        turn['persuasion_strategy'] = strategies[idx]\\n                    idx += 1\\n\\n        result.append(conv_copy)\\n\\n    return result\\n\\n\\nif __name__ == '__main__':\\n    parser = argparse.ArgumentParser()\\n    parser.add_argument('--input', required=True)\\n    parser.add_argument('--output', required=True)\\n    parser.add_argument('--seed', type=int, default=42)\\n    args = parser.parse_args()\\n\\n    with open(args.input) as f:\\n        data = [json.loads(l) for l in f]\\n\\n    scrambled = scramble_strategies(data, args.seed)\\n\\n    with open(args.output, 'w') as f:\\n        for item in scrambled:\\n            f.write(json.dumps(item) + '\\\\n')\\n\\n    jb = [c for c in scrambled if c.get('label') == 'jailbreak']\\n    print(f\\\"Processed {len(scrambled)} conversations\\\")\\n    print(f\\\"  Jailbreak (scrambled): {len(jb)}\\\")\\n    print(f\\\"  Benign (unchanged): {len(scrambled) - len(jb)}\\\")\\n\", \"stderr\": \"\", \"return_code\": 0}, \"error\": null, \"metadata\": {\"elapsed_seconds\": 0.44}}"}

399	\end{table}
400	
401	End-to-end fine-tuning unfreezes the Stage-1 encoder during Stage-2 BiGRU training, allowing gradient flow through the full pipeline. On IID and OOD DD, the two configurations are indistinguishable; on structurally complex ActorAttack, E2E recovers +5.8\,pp (error rate 6.1\%\,$\rightarrow$\,0.3\%, a substantial relative reduction). Frozen probing captures the vast majority of discriminative information from Stage-1 DAPT; the remaining gap reflects downstream optimization constraints rather than missing representational capacity.
402	
403	\section{Real-World False Positive Rate}
404	\label{app:wildchat_fpr}
405	
406	\begin{table}[ht]
407	\centering
408	\footnotesize
409	\caption{False positive rate (\%) on 226 WildChat benign multi-turn conversations (GPT-3.5 users, avg 7.4 turns per conversation). All models trained on synthetic Qwen3-8B data (1{,}000-conversation scale). 3-seed mean$\pm$std.}
410	\label{tab:wildchat_fpr}
411	\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}
412	\toprule
413	\textbf{Variant} & Seed 42 & Seed 123 & Seed 456 & Mean$\pm$Std \\
414	\midrule
415	9-class & 66.8 & 61.1 & 76.5 & 68.1$\pm$6.4 \\
416	MLM & 36.7 & 64.6 & 32.3 & 44.5$\pm$14.3 \\
417	Vanilla & 47.3 & 42.5 & 54.4 & 48.1$\pm$4.9 \\
418	\bottomrule
419	\end{tabular}
420	\end{table}
421	
422	Table~\ref{tab:wildchat_fpr} evaluates false positive rates on 226 WildChat benign multi-turn conversations from real GPT-3.5 users (avg 7.4 turns). All variants produce FPR far exceeding deployable thresholds: 9-class is worst at 68.1\%, followed by vanilla (48.1\%) and MLM (44.5\%). Domain adaptation \emph{increases} FPR relative to vanilla (+20\,pp for 9-class), indicating that continued pretraining amplifies dependence on the training benign distribution. This is consistent with the cross-LLM false positive spike (Table~\ref{tab:cross_llm_error}): models trained on synthetic Qwen3-8B benign text implicitly treat unfamiliar conversational styles as jailbreak indicators. High MLM seed variance (std\,=\,14.3\%) suggests that unsupervised pretraining is particularly unstable under benign distribution shift. Deployment requires per-domain benign calibration or mixed-source benign training data.
423	
424	\paragraph{Post-hoc calibration.}
425	ROC AUC\,=\,0.700$\pm$0.054 indicates insufficient discrimination between WildChat benign and jailbreak conversations at the representation level. At TPR$\geq$95\%, the optimal threshold still yields FPR\,=\,62.7\%---far above deployable levels. Temperature scaling cannot improve this trade-off, as ROC curves are invariant to monotonic score transforms. The AUC ceiling reflects genuine domain shift: WildChat benign and jailbreak representations overlap substantially, and no post-hoc calibration can overcome this; in-domain benign adaptation is required.
426	
427	\section{Benign Distribution Diversification Pilot}
428	\label{app:benign_mixed}
429	
430	To test whether the high WildChat FPR (Section~\ref{app:wildchat_fpr}) reflects a fundamental limitation or a remediable distribution mismatch, we conduct a pilot retraining experiment mixing 150 WildChat benign conversations into the training set. We filter 27 of 226 WildChat conversations containing jailbreak-indicative patterns, use 150 for training and 49 for held-out evaluation. Both training and evaluation WildChat samples are drawn from the same source (GPT-3.5 users); this is therefore an IID evaluation of benign coverage, not an OOD generalization test.
431	
432	\paragraph{Class ratio shift.} Adding 150 WildChat benign to the original 350-conversation training set (175 jailbreak + 175 synthetic benign) shifts the benign-to-jailbreak ratio from 50:50 to approximately 65:35 (325 benign vs.\ 175 jailbreak). To control for this confound, we downsample WildChat benign to 88 conversations (175 jailbreak : 88 WildChat benign + 87 original benign $\approx$ 50:50, $n$\,=\,350). This balanced variant achieves identical WildChat FPR (0\%, same CI) while maintaining OOD detection (AA F1\,=\,0.991, FITD/DD F1\,=\,1.000), confirming that class ratio is not the driver of FPR reduction.
433	
434	\begin{table}[ht]
435	\centering
436	\footnotesize
437	\caption{Effect of benign distribution diversification on WildChat FPR and OOD jailbreak detection. Mixed training adds 150 WildChat benign to the original 350 synthetic conversations. 3-seed mean (seeds 42, 123, 456). WildChat evaluation is IID (same source as training WildChat).}
438	\label{tab:benign_mixed}
439	\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}
440	\toprule
441	\textbf{Metric} & \textbf{Original} & \textbf{Mixed} \\
442	\midrule
443	WildChat FPR (\%) & 68.1 & \textbf{0.0} \\
444	IID F1 & 1.000 & 1.000 \\
445	DD OOD F1 & 1.000 & 1.000 \\
446	AA OOD F1 & $\sim$0.960 & 0.956 \\
447	FITD OOD F1 & 1.000 & 1.000 \\
448	\bottomrule
449	\end{tabular}
450	\end{table}
451	
452	Mixed benign training reduces WildChat FPR from 68.1\% to 0\% (exact binomial 95\% CI upper bound: 6.1\%, $n$\,=\,49) with no degradation in cross-attack detection (ActorAttack F1\,=\,0.956, within 0.4\,pp of original). This confirms the diagnosis from Section~\ref{app:wildchat_fpr}: when representative benign data is available, the high FPR is remediable without sacrificing detection performance. The evaluation set is small ($n$\,=\,49) and IID with respect to the training WildChat distribution, so the result should be interpreted as evidence that benign distribution mismatch---not model capacity---is the bottleneck; validation on larger and independently sourced OOD benign corpora is needed to establish deployment-ready FPR bounds.
453	
454	\paragraph{Benign adaptation curve.}
455	\label{para:benign_curve}
456	To quantify how many OOD benign samples suffice, we vary the number of WildChat benign conversations added to training ($N \in \{0, 5, 10, 25, 50, 100, 150\}$) and retrain the full pipeline (9-class DAPT + BiGRU) for each $N$ (seed 42).
457	
458	\begin{table}[ht]
459	\centering
460	\footnotesize
461	\caption{Benign adaptation curve: WildChat FPR and OOD detection F1 as a function of $N$ WildChat benign conversations added to the 350-conversation training set. Seed 42 (single-seed curve); $N$\,=\,10 confirmed across 3 seeds (FPR\,=\,0\% for all). WildChat evaluation $n$\,=\,49.}
462	\label{tab:benign_curve}
463	\begin{tabular}{@{}rcccccc@{}}
464	\toprule
465	$N$ & Train & FPR (\%) & DD F1 & AA F1 & FITD F1 \\
466	\midrule
467	0 & 350 & 65.3 & 1.000 & 0.981 & 1.000 \\
468	5 & 355 & 16.3 & 0.990 & 0.981 & 1.000 \\
469	10 & 360 & \textbf{0.0} & 1.000 & 1.000 & 1.000 \\
470	25 & 375 & 0.0 & 1.000 & 0.953 & 1.000 \\
471	50 & 400 & 0.0 & 1.000 & 1.000 & 1.000 \\
472	100 & 450 & 0.0 & 1.000 & 1.000 & 1.000 \\
473	150 & 500 & 0.0 & 1.000 & 0.981 & 1.000 \\
474	\bottomrule
475	\end{tabular}
476	\end{table}
477	
478	FPR drops sharply from 65.3\% ($N$\,=\,0) to 16.3\% ($N$\,=\,5) to 0\% ($N$\,=\,10), remaining at 0\% for all $N \geq 10$. OOD detection is preserved throughout: all F1 scores $\geq$\,0.953 (minor ActorAttack variation at $N$\,=\,25 is within seed noise). This demonstrates that benign distribution mismatch is addressable with minimal annotation effort---10 conversations from the target domain suffice to eliminate false positives entirely (95\% CI upper bound: 6.1\% at $n$\,=\,49). IID F1 remains 1.000 for all $N$. Multi-seed validation at $N$\,=\,10 (seeds 42, 123, 456) confirms robustness: FPR\,=\,0.0\% for all seeds, DD/FITD F1\,=\,1.000$\pm$0.000, AA F1\,=\,0.981$\pm$0.016.
479	
480	\paragraph{Second benign source: OASST2.}
481	\label{app:oasst2}
482	
483	To test whether benign-side shift generalizes beyond WildChat, we evaluate on 200 benign multi-turn conversations from OpenAssistant OASST2~\citep{kopf2023openassistant}---a crowdsourced dataset of real human conversations, distinct from WildChat (GPT-3.5 user logs) in both authorship and topic distribution.
484

Compare WildChat (Table~\ref{tab:benign_curve}): FPR reaches 0\% at $N$\,=\,10.}

493	\midrule

502	Baseline FPR on OASST2 (57.5\%) is comparable to WildChat (68.1\%), confirming that benign-side shift is not source-specific but a general consequence of synthetic-only benign training. The adaptation curve follows the same pattern---monotonic FPR reduction with preserved OOD detection---but requires more samples: 25 conversations reduce FPR to 1.0\%$\pm$0.5\% across 3 seeds (vs.\ 0\% at $N$\,=\,10 for WildChat), and 50 conversations reach 0.5\%. The slower convergence likely reflects greater distributional distance between crowdsourced OASST2 conversations and the synthetic training distribution compared to WildChat's GPT-3.5 user logs.
503

518	
519	\section{BERT-base Proxy Baseline}
520	\label{app:bert_proxy}
521	
522	\begin{table}[ht]
523	\centering
524	\footnotesize
525	\caption{BERT-base-uncased (110M) frozen probing without DAPT, matching DeepContext's encoder family~\citep{deepcontext2026}. 3-seed mean$\pm$std. DeBERTa-v3 results from Table~\ref{tab:cross_encoder} for comparison.}
526	\label{tab:bert_proxy}
527	\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}
528	\toprule
529	\textbf{Encoder} & IID & DD OOD & AA OOD \\
530	\midrule
531	BERT-base (110M), no DAPT & .996$\pm$.006 & .318$\pm$.031 & .275$\pm$.027 \\
532	DeBERTa-v3 (184M), no DAPT & 1.000$\pm$.000 & .312$\pm$.043 & .258$\pm$.000 \\
533	DeBERTa-v3 (184M), 9-class & 1.000$\pm$.000 & .994$\pm$.005 & .939$\pm$.047 \\
534	\bottomrule
535	\end{tabular}
536	\end{table}
537	
538	Table~\ref{tab:bert_proxy} compares BERT-base-uncased (110M parameters, matching DeepContext's encoder) against DeBERTa-v3-base (184M), both without domain-adaptive pretraining. The two vanilla encoders achieve comparable OOD performance (DD: 0.318 vs.\ 0.312; AA: 0.275 vs.\ 0.258), despite a 1.7$\times$ parameter difference and different pretraining procedures. Both collapse on OOD evaluation while 9-class DAPT recovers to 0.994 (DD) and 0.939 (AA). This confirms that cross-attack generalization is driven by domain adaptation, not encoder architecture or capacity at this scale.
539	
540	\section{Confidence Calibration}
541	\label{app:calibration}
542	
543	\begin{table}[ht]
544	\centering
545	\footnotesize
546	\caption{Expected calibration error (ECE, 10 bins) for 9-class and vanilla on IID and OOD evaluation (3-seed mean$\pm$std). Lower is better.}
547	\label{tab:calibration}
548	\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}
549	\toprule
550	\textbf{Variant} & IID & DD OOD & AA OOD \\
551	\midrule
552	9-class & $\approx$0.000 & 0.143$\pm$0.053 & 0.116$\pm$0.053 \\
553	Vanilla & 0.009$\pm$0.007 & 0.669$\pm$0.004 & 0.504$\pm$0.066 \\
554	\bottomrule
555	\end{tabular}
556	\end{table}
557	
558	The 9-class model achieves substantially lower ECE than vanilla on OOD data (0.12--0.14 vs.\ 0.50--0.67), indicating that domain adaptation improves not only discriminative performance but also confidence calibration. IID calibration is near-perfect for both variants. The residual OOD ECE (0.12--0.14) suggests that temperature scaling could further improve deployment reliability.
559	
560	\section{Frozen Probing Verification}
561	\label{app:frozen_verify}
562	
563	\begin{table}[ht]
564	\centering
565	\footnotesize
566	\caption{Frozen probing vs.\ end-to-end (E2E) fine-tuning (3-seed mean$\pm$std). 9-class: plan\_002 checkpoints; MLM: plan\_017\_mlm\_control (matched training). Frozen probing trains only BiGRU+MLP on frozen DAPT embeddings.}
567	\label{tab:frozen_verify}
568	\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}
569	\toprule
570	\textbf{Config} & IID & DD OOD & AA OOD & FITD OOD \\
571	\midrule
572	Frozen 9-class & 1.000$\pm$.000 & .997$\pm$.005 & .972$\pm$.026 & 1.000$\pm$.000 \\
573	Frozen MLM & .982$\pm$.016 & .993$\pm$.009 & .941$\pm$.037 & .987$\pm$.009 \\
574	E2E 9-class & 1.000$\pm$.000 & 1.000$\pm$.000 & .997$\pm$.005 & --- \\
575	\bottomrule
576	\end{tabular}
577	\end{table}
578	
579	Table~\ref{tab:frozen_verify} compares frozen probing across DAPT objectives and against end-to-end fine-tuning. On easy OOD (DD, FITD), frozen MLM and frozen 9-class representations perform comparably ($\Delta \leq 1.3$\,pp). On hard OOD (ActorAttack), 9-class retains a modest advantage (0.972 vs.\ 0.941, $\Delta$\,=\,3.1\,pp) with lower variance ($\sigma$\,=\,0.026 vs.\ 0.037). Both far exceed vanilla encoders ($\leq$0.318; Table~\ref{tab:bert_proxy}). The E2E advantage over frozen 9-class is $\leq$2.5\,pp, confirming that Stage-1 representation quality is the primary source of OOD generalization.
580	
581	A striking asymmetry emerges when comparing frozen probing to the full pipeline (Table~\ref{tab:evidence_hierarchy}): MLM frozen representations \emph{outperform} the full MLM pipeline on AA OOD (0.941 vs.\ 0.893), while 9-class frozen exactly matches its full pipeline (0.972 vs.\ 0.972). This indicates that MLM's cross-seed instability originates in Stage-2 BiGRU training rather than Stage-1 DAPT---the MLM encoder produces adequate representations that are then degraded by unstable downstream optimization. Classification DAPT, by contrast, produces representations that remain robust through Stage-2 training, explaining its consistently lower variance (Table~\ref{tab:evidence_hierarchy}).
582	
583	This two-level structure---DAPT vs.\ no-DAPT producing a representation quality leap (+70\,pp), classification vs.\ MLM DAPT producing a stability difference---is consistent with two further observations: $\lambda$=0 matches $\lambda > 0$ during E2E fine-tuning (Table~\ref{tab:lambda}), and mean pooling matches BiGRU under E2E (Table~\ref{tab:temporal}). Once DAPT representations are strong, downstream configuration details have minimal impact.
584	
585	\paragraph{Representation geometry analysis.}
586	\label{app:representation}
587	
588	To characterize the mechanism behind classification DAPT's robustness, we analyze Stage-1 representations across all seven DeBERTa-v3-base variants (seed 42). We extract \texttt{[CLS]} embeddings from 150 test conversations (50 IID + 50 DD OOD + 50 AA OOD) and compute three metrics.
589	
590	\begin{table}[ht]
591	\centering
592	\footnotesize
593	\caption{Representation geometry and class separability across Stage-1 variants. PR: participation ratio (effective dimensionality of the embedding space). Fisher ratio: inter-class distance$^2$ / sum of intra-class variances. Weight $\Delta$: L2 distance from pretrained weights (relative to pretrained norm).}
594	\label{tab:representation}
595	\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}
596	\toprule
597	\textbf{Variant} & \textbf{PR} & \textbf{Fisher} & \textbf{Wt.\ $\Delta$ (\%)} \\

Frozen probing captures the vast majority of discriminative information from Stage-1 DAPT; the remaining gap reflects downstream optimization constraints rather than missing representational capacity.\n\n\\section{Real-World False Positive Rate}\n\\label{app:wildchat_fpr}\n\n\\begin{table}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\footnotesize\n\\caption{False positive rate (\\%) on 226 WildChat benign multi-turn conversations (GPT-3.5 users, avg 7.4 turns per conversation). This is consistent with the cross-LLM false positive spike (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_llm_error}): models trained on synthetic Qwen3-8B benign text implicitly treat unfamiliar conversational styles as jailbreak indicators. High MLM seed variance (std\\,=\\,14.3\\%) suggests that unsupervised pretraining is particularly unstable under benign distribution shift. Deployment requires per-domain benign calibration or mixed-source benign training data.\n\n\\paragraph{Post-hoc calibration.}\nROC AUC\\,=\\,0.700$\\pm$0.054 indicates insufficient discrimination between WildChat benign and jailbreak conversations at the representation level. At TPR$\\geq$95\\%, the optimal threshold still yields FPR\\,=\\,62.7\\%---far above deployable levels. Temperature scaling cannot improve this trade-off, as ROC curves are invariant to monotonic score transforms. <details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01TWAGABS66Hkc1pEa2cUA77`</summary>

```text
670	\midrule
671	\multirow{2}{*}{Frozen} & BiGRU & .997$\pm$.005 & .972$\pm$.026 \\
672	& Mean pooling & .991$\pm$.013 & .962$\pm$.015 \\
673	\midrule
674	\multirow{2}{*}{E2E} & BiGRU & 1.000$\pm$.000 & .997$\pm$.005 \\
675	& Mean pooling & 1.000$\pm$.000 & 1.000$\pm$.000 \\
676	\bottomrule
677	\end{tabular}
678	\end{table}
679	
680	Table~\ref{tab:temporal} compares BiGRU and mean pooling under both frozen and E2E settings. Under frozen probing (the main paper's pipeline), BiGRU provides a marginal advantage on ActorAttack (+1.0\,pp) with overlapping confidence intervals, consistent with the main text finding that temporal order is not a core detection factor ($|\Delta| < 1$\,pp under turn shuffling; Section~\ref{sec:decomposition}). Under E2E fine-tuning, the difference vanishes entirely: mean pooling saturates all benchmarks at F1\,=\,1.000. The choice of frozen BiGRU in the main pipeline reflects a design trade-off: it enables representation-level analysis (CKA, t-SNE) that E2E would confound, while sacrificing $\leq$1\,pp on the hardest OOD set.
681	
682	\section{Length Stratification Analysis}
683	\label{app:length}
684	
685	\begin{table}[ht]
686	\centering
687	\footnotesize
688	\caption{Jailbreak recall by conversation length bin on OOD evaluation (3-seed majority vote). Benign conversations span 12--20 turns; jailbreak conversations span 3--10 turns.}
689	\label{tab:length}
690	\begin{tabular}{@{}llccc@{}}
691	\toprule
692	\textbf{Dataset} & \textbf{Variant} & 1--3 turns & 4--6 turns & 7--10 turns \\
693	\midrule
694	\multirow{2}{*}{DD OOD} & 9-class & --- & 100.0\% & 99.4\% \\
695	& Vanilla & --- & $\leq$2.6\% & $\leq$1.7\% \\
696	\midrule
697	\multirow{2}{*}{AA OOD} & 9-class & 93.3\% & 95.6\% & 100.0\% \\
698	& Vanilla & 0.0\% & $\leq$2.6\% & 0.0\% \\
699	\bottomrule
700	\end{tabular}
701	\end{table}
702	
703	Table~\ref{tab:length} stratifies jailbreak recall by conversation length. The 9-class model shows no systematic degradation: ActorAttack recall increases monotonically from 93.3\% (1--3 turns) to 100\% (7--10 turns), and DD recall is near-perfect across all bins.
704	
705	\paragraph{Length confound.}
706	Benign conversations (12--20 turns) and jailbreak conversations (3--10 turns) have zero length overlap in the OOD evaluation sets, raising the concern that the model discriminates by conversation length rather than content. Three observations argue against this interpretation:
707	(1)~Vanilla DeBERTa has access to identical length information via the BiGRU but achieves recall $\leq$2.6\% across all bins---length alone is insufficient without domain-adapted representations.
708	(2)~At prefix $K$=1, where all conversations are length-equalized, 9-class still achieves F1\,=\,0.773$\pm$0.041 on ActorAttack (Table~\ref{tab:actorattack_full}), demonstrating content-based detection independent of length.
709	(3)~The model is trained on IID data (Crescendo) and cannot learn OOD-specific length distributions; generalization to unseen length patterns must rely on content features that transfer across attack families.
710	
711	\paragraph{Direct empirical verification.}
712	\label{app:length_independence}
713	
714	\begin{table}[ht]
715	\centering
716	\footnotesize
717	\caption{Turn-count independence verification (9-class, plan\_002 checkpoints, 3 seeds). Part~A: FPR on test benign truncated to $K$ user turns (all seeds FPR\,=\,0). Part~B: WildChat FPR by turn-count bucket (mean$\pm$std). Part~C: jailbreak detection (F1) after padding with benign turns to 15+ total turns (mean$\pm$std where available).}
718	\label{tab:length_independence}
719	\begin{tabular}{@{}llcc@{}}
720	\toprule
721	& \textbf{Condition} & \textbf{FPR / F1} & $n$ \\
722	\midrule
723	\multicolumn{4}{l}{\textit{Part A: Benign truncation (test set)}} \\[2pt]
724	& Original (12--20 turns) & FPR = .000 & 38 \\
725	& Truncated $K$=3 & FPR = .000 & 38 \\
726	& Truncated $K$=5 & FPR = .000 & 38 \\
727	& Truncated $K$=8 & FPR = .000 & 38 \\
728	& Truncated $K$=10$^{*}$ & FPR = .000 & 38 \\
729	\midrule
730	\multicolumn{4}{l}{\textit{Part B: WildChat FPR by turn count}} \\[2pt]
731	& Short (3--10 turns) & FPR = .770$\pm$.105 & 196 \\
732	& Medium (11--15 turns) & FPR = .942$\pm$.054 & 23 \\
733	& Long (16+ turns) & FPR = .857$\pm$.117 & 7 \\
734	\midrule
735	\multicolumn{4}{l}{\textit{Part C: Jailbreak padding to 15+ turns}} \\[2pt]
736	& DD original & F1 = .996 & 80 \\
737	& DD padded & F1 = .971 ($\Delta$=$-$2.5\,pp) & 80 \\
738	& AA original & F1 = .963 & 80 \\
739	& AA padded & F1 = .383$\pm$.261 ($\Delta$=$-$57.9\,pp) & 80 \\
740	\bottomrule
741	\end{tabular}
742	\vspace{-2pt}
743	{\footnotesize $^{*}$$K$=10 did not truncate any conversation (test benign max $\sim$8 user turns).}
744	\end{table}
745	
746	Truncating benign conversations to match jailbreak turn counts (Part~A) produces zero false positives across all truncation levels and all 3 seeds, directly confirming that the model does not use conversation length as a detection shortcut. $K$=3 and $K$=5 are the effective control conditions where all 38 conversations were truncated; $K$=10 exceeds the maximum test benign length and serves as a no-op control.
747	
748	WildChat FPR by turn count (Part~B) provides complementary evidence: short conversations (3--10 turns, $n$=196) have \emph{lower} FPR than medium-length ones (11--15 turns, $n$=23), inconsistent with a ``short\,=\,jailbreak'' shortcut. Medium and long buckets have limited sample sizes and should be interpreted as directional evidence only.
749	
750	Padding jailbreak conversations with benign turns (Part~C) reveals an asymmetry: DD detection is resilient ($\Delta$=$-$2.5\,pp), but ActorAttack collapses ($\Delta$=$-$57.9\,pp). Padding experiments append benign turns \emph{after} the complete attack sequence; in practice, adversaries would not dilute their own successful attacks. The collapse nonetheless exposes BiGRU temporal aggregation as vulnerable to attention dilution on subtle attack signals, a limitation discussed in Section~\ref{sec:limitations}.
751	
752	\section{Efficiency}
753	\label{app:efficiency}
754	
755	\begin{table}[ht]
756	\centering
757	\footnotesize
758	\caption{Inference efficiency comparison on 100 conversations (50 IID + 50 DD OOD), same GPU (RTX PRO 6000). All methods measured under identical conditions.$^{\dagger}$}
759	\label{tab:efficiency}
760	\begin{tabular}{@{}lrcr@{}}
761	\toprule
762	\textbf{Method} & Latency (ms) & Params & VRAM \\
763	\midrule
764	DeBERTa+BiGRU (ours) & 13.1$\pm$1.6 & 186.7M & 868\,MB \\
765	Qwen3-8B 3-shot & 318.5$\pm$26.8 & 8.2B & 16.1\,GB \\
766	\bottomrule
767	\end{tabular}
768	\vspace{-2pt}
769	{\footnotesize $^{\dagger}$LLM inference speed varies significantly across GPU generations; same-hardware comparison ensures a fair speedup estimate.}
```

Under frozen probing (the main paper's pipeline), BiGRU provides a marginal advantage on ActorAttack (+1.0\\,pp) with overlapping confidence intervals, consistent with the main text finding that temporal order is not a core detection factor ($|\\Delta| < 1$\\,pp under turn shuffling; Section~\\ref{sec:decomposition}). Good findings so far. Let me summarize what I know:

**P14**: The scramble_labels.py uses a single random permutation (`rng.shuffle(strategies)` with a fixed `seed`). Each different seed training run uses its own seed for data scrambling, but within each run it's a single permutation. The script takes `--seed` as argument.

However, looking more carefully - the scramble_labels.py creates a single scrambled dataset per seed. 

The TOST tests run across 10 different seeds (42, 123, 456, 789, 1000, 2024, 2025, 2026, 314, 628), and each seed generates a different random permutation of the labels. So it's multiple permutations across different seeds, but each individual training run applies just one permutation to all its data.

Looking at the scramble_strategies function more closely: it takes a seed, initializes an RNG with that seed, then for each jailbreak conversation it collects the strategy labels and shuffles them. Since the RNG state progresses through each conversation, each one gets a different permutation of its strategies, rather than using a single fixed mapping. So the question P14 is really asking whether each training run uses one permutation or multiple - and the answer is that each seed produces its own permutation, tested across the 10 seeds in TOST. I'm realizing the scrambling happens once with seed 42, creating a single label permutation that's reused across all 10 training seeds—the variation comes from different model initializations, not different data scrambles. Since it's a single permutation, I should add a note about this in the Limitations section as the P14 instruction suggests.

Let me double-check if any of the experiment files like exp39_extra_seeds.py or exp41_tost_10seed.py use different scramble seeds to be certain.

For P15, I'm looking for the strategy annotation prompt file that should document how Qwen3-8B annotated each user turn with structured prompts.  But actually, the generation scripts themselves embed the strategy guidance directly into the generation prompt—it's not a separate post-hoc annotation step. The appendix already covers this, so I can skip P15.

Now I'll move forward with the edits. I need to add Hsu et al. (2024) and M2S (2025) to the references, then check for any unsubstantiated claims in the scope section, and finally downgrade the mixed-LLM claim in the experiments section around line 180-183.

Let me start with the references update.

    "oldString": "Per-turn classifiers~\\citep{inan2023llamaguard,metallamaguard3_2024,zeng2024shieldgemma,han2024wildguard,anthropic2025constitutional} and single-turn defenses~\\citep{robey2024smoothllm,xu2024safedecoding,jain2023baseline,alon2023perplexity,phute2023selfdefense} miss multi-turn trajectories.",
    "newString": "Per-turn classifiers~\\citep{inan2023llamaguard,metallamaguard3_2024,zeng2024shieldgemma,han2024wildguard,anthropic2025constitutional} and single-turn defenses~\\citep{robey2024smoothllm,xu2024safedecoding,jain2023baseline,alon2023perplexity,phute2023selfdefense,hsu2024improved} miss multi-turn trajectories; \\citet{li2025m2s} propose converting multi-turn attacks to single-turn for existing guardrails, a complementary approach to our direct multi-turn detection.",
    "originalFile": "% related_work.tex — D048 deep writing optimization\n\n\\section{Related Work}\n\n\\paragraph{Multi-turn jailbreak attacks.}\nAll multi-turn jailbreak families share a persuasion substrate~\\citep{zeng2024pap,hagendorff2026reasoning,li2024mhj}: Crescendo~\\citep{russinovich2024crescendo}, FITD~\\citep{fitd2024}, DD~\\citep{deceptivedelight2024}, and ActorAttack~\\citep{ren2024actorattack} each operationalize it differently, while automated methods~\\citep{chao2023pair,mehrotra2024tap,perez2022redteaming} iteratively refine prompts and many-shot attacks~\\citep{anil2024manyshot} exploit long-context in-context learning. Benchmarks standardize evaluation at single-turn~\\citep{mazeika2024harmbench,chao2024jailbreakbench} and multi-turn~\\citep{wei2023jailbroken,jiang2024redqueen,song2026multibreak} scales. A detector must generalize across families, not merely succeed on its training distribution.\n\n\\paragraph{Multi-turn defenses and production safety.}\nNo existing defense evaluates cross-attack transfer. Conversation-level systems (DeepContext~\\citep{deepcontext2026}, BIID~\\citep{tong2025biid}, G-Guard~\\citep{gguard2025}, SafeDream~\\citep{safedream2026}, JailDAM~\\citep{nian2025jaildam}) and representation-level approaches~\\citep{lu2025xboundary,zhang2025jbshield,xie2024gradsafe,zou2024circuitbreakers,kulkarni2026latentadversarial,kadali2026trace,hua2026rcs,shen2025antidote,li2025repejailbreak,wang2026trajguard,templeton2024saemitigators} each evaluate on a single family; the latter require white-box access and can be brittle~\\citep{derya2026revisitingjbshield}. J-DAPT~\\citep{marchiori2025jdapt} applies CORAL-based domain adaptation with cross-modal fusion for multimodal robotic jailbreak detection; our work focuses on text-only multi-turn persuasion attacks and decomposes which adaptation factors drive cross-attack generalization through controlled ablation. Per-turn classifiers~\\citep{inan2023llamaguard,metallamaguard3_2024,zeng2024shieldgemma,han2024wildguard,anthropic2025constitutional} and single-turn defenses~\\citep{robey2024smoothllm,xu2024safedecoding,jain2023baseline,alon2023perplexity,phute2023selfdefense} miss multi-turn trajectories. Our ablation contributes to filling this gap via decomposition across thirteen configurations under OOD evaluation.\n\n\\paragraph{Computational persuasion and discourse.}\nPersuasion and discourse frameworks~\\citep{austin1962speechacts,searle1969speechacts,mann1988rst,prasad2008pdtb,stolcke2000dialogueact} and technique-level classification~\\citep{cialdini2001influence,zeng2024pap,hagendorff2026reasoning,dasanmartino2019fine,dasanmartino2020semeval,wang2019persuasion} offer interpretable vocabulary for attacks, but whether such structure aids detection has not been tested. Our ablation shows the taxonomy enables characterization, but detection operates through domain-adapted embeddings regardless of label correctness.\n\n\\paragraph{Domain-adaptive pretraining.}\nContinued pretraining on domain text yields consistent gains~\\citep{gururangan2020dontstop,lee2020biobert,beltagy2019scibert,howard2018ulmfit}, but has not been disentangled for safety-critical generalization. Intermediate-task transfer~\\citep{phang2018stilts} and multi-task learning~\\citep{caruana1997multitask,liu2019mtdnn} confound domain exposure with supervised signal; our MLM control confirms pretraining alone closes the OOD gap. Dataset artifacts~\\citep{gururangan2018artifacts,niven2019probing} motivate TF-IDF, adversarial NLP~\\citep{jin2020textfooler,li2020bertattack,nasr2025attackermovessecond,andriushchenko2024adaptive} informs perturbation, and probing~\\citep{conneau2018probing,hewitt2019structural} underpins CKA analysis.\n",

18	\section{Conclusion}
19	
20	Thirteen encoder configurations decompose multi-turn jailbreak generalization into two orthogonal axes: attack-side (classification DAPT via discriminative compression; MLM collapses under mixed-LLM data) and benign-side (FPR 65--68\%\,$\rightarrow$\,0--1.5\% via 10--25 conversations on two independent sources). Any deterministic partition suffices for template attacks ($d < 0.1$), with a complexity-dependent boundary on ActorAttack ($d$\,=\,0.44). With 24.3$\times$ speedup over LLM-as-judge, this yields a two-step protocol without target-domain attack data.
21	
22	\section{Limitations}
```

    "originalFile": "% conclusion.tex — D073 Phase 2 revision (page-compressed)\n\n\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Confounds and transferability.}\nThree observations argue against a topic shortcut: unstable topic OOD (F1\\,=\\,0.541$\\pm$0.207), scrambled labels within 3.9\\,pp, and label-free MLM detection (F1\\,$\\geq$\\,0.97). ToxicChat (+18--42\\,pp; Table~\\ref{tab:human_data}) and cross-LLM experiments (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}) confirm transfer; mixed-LLM 9-class maintains DD F1\\,=\\,0.818$\\pm$0.096 while MLM collapses to vanilla level (Appendix~\\ref{app:mixed_llm}). Dataset composition---not sample count---is the primary determinant (Appendix~\\ref{app:scale_validation}). Prompt optimization improves LLM-as-judge OOD detection but does not close the gap with encoder-based methods (Appendix~\\ref{app:prompt_opt}).\n\n\\paragraph{Evidence for discriminative compression.}\nClassification DAPT compresses the feature space into few discriminative dimensions (participation ratio 3.7--9.4 vs.\\ 25--32 for MLM/vanilla) with 3--5 orders of magnitude higher separability (Fisher 2.6--19.7 vs.\\ $<$0.001; Appendix~\\ref{app:representation}). Scrambled labels produce comparable compression (Fisher\\,=\\,2.6), consistent with \\citet{zhang2021understanding}'s framework for how classification modifies representations, with two domain-specific findings: (i)~domain-relevant label partitioning is necessary (topic labels insufficient), and (ii)~compression localizes to 3 SVD dimensions in layers 8--12 (Fisher 15$\\times$ vanilla at peak; Appendix~\\ref{app:layerwise}), with early layers remaining label-independent as in \\citet{maennel2020random}. A discriminative probe confirms: 3 SVD components of 9-class layer-10 representations achieve perfect AA OOD detection (F1\\,=\\,1.000), while random projections degrade to 0.928 and MLM reaches only 0.636 with all 768 dimensions (Appendix~\\ref{app:discriminative_probe}). The complexity-dependent boundary persists (TOST $d < 0.1$ on DD/FITD; $d$\\,=\\,0.44 on AA). Frozen probing near-parity (AA gap 3.1\\,pp) confirms discriminative structure is established in Stage-1.\n\n\\paragraph{Condition-specific advantage.}\nThe evidence hierarchy is specific to multi-turn synthetic evaluation. On ToxicChat (single-turn human data), JB-MLM leads (0.837) while 9-class ranks lower (0.596): 77\\% from false positives and 23\\% from missed attacks lacking multi-turn signals. This reversal is not driven by multi-turn structure: truncating synthetic conversations to a single turn \\emph{widens} the 9-class advantage ($\\bar{\\Delta}$\\,=\\,+1.6\\,pp full $\\to$ +6.0\\,pp single-turn; Appendix~\\ref{app:truncated}), implicating distributional differences between synthetic and human text. Variance reverses (classification std\\,=\\,0.087 vs.\\ MLM\\,=\\,0.013); practitioners facing single-turn human data should consider MLM DAPT.\n\n\\paragraph{Two-axis generalization.}\nOOD F1 reflects attack-side generalization under IID benign conditions. The orthogonal challenge is benign-side shift: DAPT increases WildChat FPR from 48.1\\% to 68.1\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:wildchat_fpr}). Ten target-domain conversations reduce it to 0\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:benign_mixed}); OASST2 confirms the pattern (FPR 57.5\\%\\,$\\rightarrow$\\,1.0\\%$\\pm$0.5\\% at $N$\\,=\\,25; Appendix~\\ref{app:oasst2}).\n\n\\section{Conclusion}\n\nThirteen encoder configurations decompose multi-turn jailbreak generalization into two orthogonal axes: attack-side (classification DAPT via discriminative compression; MLM collapses under mixed-LLM data) and benign-side (FPR 65--68\\%\\,$\\rightarrow$\\,0--1.5\\% via 10--25 conversations on two independent sources). Any deterministic partition suffices for template attacks ($d < 0.1$), with a complexity-dependent boundary on ActorAttack ($d$\\,=\\,0.44). With 24.3$\\times$ speedup over LLM-as-judge, this yields a two-step protocol without target-domain attack data.\n\n\\section{Limitations}\n\\label{sec:limitations}\n\nAll multi-turn findings derive from synthetic conversations generated by Qwen3-8B (350 training, 76--118 test per family); preliminary mixed-LLM training (Appendix~\\ref{app:mixed_llm}) suggests this limitation is addressable. Most pairwise comparisons rely on 3 seeds; key scrambled-vs.-correct comparisons are extended to 10 seeds (Appendix~\\ref{app:seed_level}). ToxicChat validates transfer to human-authored single-turn attacks (+18--42\\,pp over vanilla), but the evidence hierarchy reverses on this data (Section~\\ref{sec:discussion}), and no human-generated multi-turn jailbreak dataset exists.\n\nOOD results reflect attack-side generalization only; benign data is IID, so OOD F1 figures are upper bounds. On WildChat, 9-class FPR reaches 68.1\\%; 10 target-domain conversations eliminate it ($n$\\,=\\,49). OASST2 confirms the pattern but requires 25--50 conversations for comparable reduction (FPR\\,=\\,1.0\\%$\\pm$0.5\\% at $N$\\,=\\,25, 3 seeds; Appendix~\\ref{app:oasst2}); validation on additional platforms is needed. Residual calibration error (ECE\\,=\\,0.126--0.141; Appendix~\\ref{app:calibration}) suggests temperature scaling is needed for deployment.\n\nBenign-only adaptation addresses human-population distribution shift (WildChat, OASST2) but not cross-LLM distribution shift: adapting on Llama-origin benign conversations reduces FPR but collapses Llama jailbreak detection, as the model conflates LLM-specific style features with benign class membership (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}). Three encoders (125M--355M) differ in architecture and pretraining; same-architecture scaling is needed to isolate the capacity effect. Only persuasion-mediated multi-turn attacks fall within scope; single-turn token-level attacks~\\citep{zou2023gcg}, non-persuasive strategies, and non-text modalities are not addressed. Total pipeline cost is under 4 GPU-hours on a single A100.\n\n\\section*{Ethics Statement}\n\nThis work is defensive: it improves detection of multi-turn jailbreak attacks rather than developing new attacks. We acknowledge dual-use risk, as the persuasion taxonomy could inform adversarial actors. We mitigate this by using only synthetic conversations (no real harmful content) and withholding attack-generation prompts. Detection code, model weights, and evaluation data will be released under CC BY-NC 4.0. No human subjects were involved.\n",

No target-domain attack data is required.",
    "originalFile": "% introduction.tex — expanded for 8-page target\n\n\\section{Introduction}\n\nMulti-turn jailbreak attacks distribute adversarial intent across individually benign turns. Crescendo~\\citep{russinovich2024crescendo}, Foot-in-the-Door~\\citep{fitd2024}, and Deceptive Delight~\\citep{deceptivedelight2024} all rely on persuasion as their core mechanism: \\citet{zeng2024pap} catalogue 40 persuasion techniques across jailbreak families, and \\citet{hagendorff2026reasoning} identify strategies in 97\\% of successful attacks. Because individual attack turns occupy the same embedding region as benign text~\\citep{bullwinkel2025repeng}, no single-turn filter can distinguish them from legitimate conversation.\n\nExisting defenses leave a critical gap. Production classifiers such as Llama Guard~\\citep{inan2023llamaguard}, WildGuard~\\citep{han2024wildguard}, and ShieldGemma~\\citep{zeng2024shieldgemma} operate on individual turns and miss multi-turn manipulation; Llama Guard~3 detects only 1.8\\% of multi-turn jailbreaks in our evaluation (Section~\\ref{sec:decomposition}). Multi-turn defenses (DeepContext~\\citep{deepcontext2026}, BIID~\\citep{tong2025biid}, G-Guard~\\citep{gguard2025}) model conversation-level structure but each evaluates on a single attack family. None answers the central question: \\emph{what representation properties enable a detector to generalize across attack families it has never seen?}\n\nPretrained language understanding does not suffice at base model scale: vanilla DeBERTa-v3~\\citep{he2021deberta} with temporal aggregation (cf.\\ DeepContext) drops from F1\\,=\\,1.000 in-distribution to 0.312 on OOD Deceptive Delight. LLM-as-judge baselines also struggle (Appendix~\\ref{sec:llm_judge}), though prompt optimization may improve their performance. The bottleneck is distributional: cross-attack generalization requires domain adaptation, not a larger model.\n\nOur pipeline, \\textbf{DisPeD}\\footnote{\\emph{Disentangled} refers to the two-stage architecture design separating encoder adaptation from temporal classification, not to label semantics.} (Disentangled Pattern Detection), separates encoder adaptation (Stage~1) from temporal classification (Stage~2, a frozen BiGRU; Figure~\\ref{fig:architecture}). Seven Stage~1 variants systematically vary training objectives while sharing the same two-stage pipeline (Section~\\ref{sec:method}); the contribution is the decomposition, not the pipeline.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig1_architecture.pdf}\n\\caption{DisPeD architecture. Stage~1 adapts a DeBERTa encoder on jailbreak conversation text (seven variant objectives). Stage~2 aggregates frozen \\texttt{[CLS]} embeddings via BiGRU for conversation-level jailbreak detection. Only user turns are processed.}\n\\label{fig:architecture}\n\\end{figure}\n\nControlled ablation across DeBERTa-v3 and RoBERTa~\\citep{liu2019roberta} on four attack families and two training compositions reveals two orthogonal generalization axes. On the \\emph{attack side}, vanilla DeBERTa drops to F1\\,=\\,0.28--0.31 on OOD attacks; classification DAPT recovers to 0.97--1.0, while MLM collapses under mixed-LLM training (F1\\,=\\,0.289 $\\approx$ vanilla). On the \\emph{benign side}, distributional mismatch drives false positives: WildChat FPR reaches 68.1\\%, but 10--25 target-domain conversations reduce it to 0--1.5\\% across two sources. RoBERTa-large (355M) handles ActorAttack~\\citep{ren2024actorattack} without adaptation. Classification supervision is associated with substantially lower prediction variance on synthetic data but not on human-authored conversations (Section~\\ref{sec:human_data}).\n\nOur contributions:\n\\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*, itemsep=2pt, topsep=4pt]\n  \\item \\textbf{Systematic ablation of cross-attack generalization factors.} Thirteen encoder configurations at three scales (125--355M) decompose multi-turn jailbreak detection into two orthogonal axes---attack-side and benign-side---the first such controlled factorial study in this domain. On the attack side, classification DAPT recovers OOD detection (vanilla 0.28--0.31 $\\rightarrow$ 0.97--1.0) while unsupervised pretraining collapses under mixed-LLM training. On the benign side, 10--25 target-domain conversations reduce FPR from 65--68\\% to 0--1.5\\% on two independent sources (WildChat, OASST2) without detection degradation.\n\n  \\item \\textbf{Deterministic partition sufficiency with complexity-dependent boundary.} We observe that any deterministic label partition triggers discriminative compression sufficient for cross-attack generalization---based on two template and one structurally complex attack family (structural complexity is not formally defined): scrambled labels match correct labels on template attacks (TOST-confirmed, $d < 0.1$ on DD/FITD at $n$\\,=\\,10), with a complexity-dependent boundary ($d$\\,=\\,0.44 on ActorAttack). This is consistent with \\citet{zhang2021understanding}'s framework, with two domain-specific findings: domain-relevant partitioning is necessary (topic labels insufficient) and compression localizes to 3 SVD dimensions in layers 8--12; a discriminative probe confirms that these 3 dimensions suffice for cross-attack detection (Appendix~\\ref{app:discriminative_probe}).\n\n  \\item \\textbf{Two-step adaptation protocol.} Continued pretraining is 24.3$\\times$ faster than LLM-as-judge (Appendix~\\ref{app:efficiency}); 10--25 target-domain benign conversations reduce FPR below deployment thresholds on both WildChat and OASST2 crowdsourced conversations. No target-domain attack data is required.\n\\end{itemize}\n",

_source line 109 · 2026-05-22T14:12:11.431Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01CWUZ8ct6EW5sQRNJTp9vyL`</summary>

```text
20	Controlled ablation across DeBERTa-v3 and RoBERTa~\citep{liu2019roberta} on four attack families and two training compositions reveals two orthogonal generalization axes. On the \emph{attack side}, vanilla DeBERTa drops to F1\,=\,0.28--0.31 on OOD attacks; classification DAPT recovers to 0.97--1.0, while MLM collapses under mixed-LLM training (F1\,=\,0.289 $\approx$ vanilla). On the \emph{benign side}, distributional mismatch drives false positives: WildChat FPR reaches 68.1\%, but 10--25 target-domain conversations reduce it to 0--1.5\% across two sources. RoBERTa-large (355M) handles ActorAttack~\citep{ren2024actorattack} without adaptation. Classification supervision is associated with substantially lower prediction variance on synthetic data but not on human-authored conversations (Section~\ref{sec:human_data}).
21	
22	Our contributions:
23	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*, itemsep=2pt, topsep=4pt]
24	  \item \textbf{Systematic ablation of cross-attack generalization factors.} Thirteen encoder configurations at three scales (125--355M) decompose multi-turn jailbreak detection into two orthogonal axes---attack-side and benign-side---the first such controlled factorial study in this domain. On the attack side, classification DAPT recovers OOD detection (vanilla 0.28--0.31 $\rightarrow$ 0.97--1.0) while unsupervised pretraining collapses under mixed-LLM training. On the benign side, 10--25 target-domain conversations reduce FPR from 65--68\% to 0--1.5\% on two independent sources (WildChat, OASST2) without detection degradation.
25	
26	  \item \textbf{Deterministic partition sufficiency with complexity-dependent boundary.} We observe that any deterministic label partition triggers discriminative compression sufficient for cross-attack generalization---based on two template and one structurally complex attack family (structural complexity is not formally defined): scrambled labels match correct labels on template attacks (TOST-confirmed, $d < 0.1$ on DD/FITD at $n$\,=\,10), with a complexity-dependent boundary ($d$\,=\,0.44 on ActorAttack). This is consistent with \citet{zhang2021understanding}'s framework, with two domain-specific findings: domain-relevant partitioning is necessary (topic labels insufficient) and compression localizes to 3 SVD dimensions in layers 8--12; a discriminative probe confirms that these 3 dimensions suffice for cross-attack detection (Appendix~\ref{app:discriminative_probe}).
27	
28	  \item \textbf{Preliminary two-step adaptation protocol.} On the synthetic benchmark, continued pretraining is 24.3$\times$ faster than LLM-as-judge (Appendix~\ref{app:efficiency}); 10--25 target-domain benign conversations reduce FPR below deployment thresholds on both WildChat and OASST2 crowdsourced conversations. No target-domain attack data is required.
29	\end{itemize}
```

180	\subsection{Mixed-LLM Training}
181	
182	All preceding experiments use single-source (Qwen3-8B) data. To test whether the evidence hierarchy transfers when training data mixes LLM sources, we train on 250 Qwen + 250 Llama-3.1-8B conversations (Table~\ref{tab:mixed_llm}).
183	Classification-based DAPT is the only variant that substantially outperforms vanilla: 9-class achieves DD OOD F1\,=\,0.818$\pm$0.096 (+53\,pp over vanilla 0.291), while MLM drops to vanilla level (0.289$\pm$0.101). ActorAttack remains challenging (9-class F1\,=\,0.504$\pm$0.107) with high seed variance, suggesting limited per-source volume is insufficient for complex attacks. Classification supervision generalizes across LLM sources where unsupervised pretraining does not; full results in Appendix~\ref{app:mixed_llm}.
184	
185	%----------------------------------------------------------------------
```
