    "originalFile": "% experiments.tex — D024 compressed for 8-page limit\n\n\\section{Experiments}\n\n\\paragraph{Terminology.}\n\\emph{Cross-attack OOD}: evaluation on attack families unseen during training; benign distribution remains IID throughout (OOD reflects attack-side shift only). $K$: prefix length (number of conversation turns retained). \\emph{DAPT}: all Stage-1 adaptations, extending \\citet{gururangan2020dontstop} to supervised variants. \\emph{Evidence hierarchy}: vanilla $<$ MLM $<$ classification (Section~\\ref{sec:decomposition}).\\footnote{Throughout, OOD refers to attack-family shift (novel persuasion strategies at test time); benign distribution shift is evaluated in Section~\\ref{sec:discussion}.}\n\n\\subsection{Setup}\n\n\\paragraph{Data.}\nAll synthetic data is generated by Qwen3-8B~\\citep{qwen3_2025}.\nThe IID dataset contains 250 Crescendo~\\citep{russinovich2024crescendo} jailbreak and 250 topic-anchored benign conversations, split into training (350), validation (74), and test (76) sets with balanced class ratios (e.g., test = 38 jailbreak + 38 benign).\nStage~1 (DAPT) uses training + validation conversations; Stage~2 (BiGRU) trains on training, early-stops on validation, and evaluates on test.\nOOD evaluation pairs attack-specific jailbreak conversations with the 38 IID-test benign conversations: 80 FITD~\\citep{fitd2024} (non-discriminative; Section~\\ref{sec:decomposition}) + 38 benign = 118 total, 80 Deceptive Delight (DD)~\\citep{deceptivedelight2024} + 38 benign = 118 total, and 80 ActorAttack~\\citep{ren2024actorattack} + 38 benign = 118 total.\nNo overlap exists in attack templates across families; benign is IID (same generation pipeline).\nA paraphrased version of the IID test set (BERTScore 0.92, lexical overlap 0.20) serves as the adversarial robustness benchmark (Section~\\ref{sec:perturbation}).\nFor human data validation, we use ToxicChat~\\citep{lin2023toxicchat} (204 jailbreak + 204 benign from real LMSYS user interactions), entirely independent of Qwen3-8B generation.\n\n\\paragraph{Models.}\nSeven DeBERTa-v3 variants share the same Stage-2 pipeline and differ only in Stage-1 training (Section~\\ref{sec:method}).\nOur vanilla DeBERTa+BiGRU configuration is architecturally equivalent to DeepContext~\\citep{deepcontext2026}, differing only in encoder choice (DeBERTa-v3-base vs.\\ BERT); it serves as our primary baseline.\nFor cross-architecture comparison, we replicate vanilla, MLM, and 9-class variants on RoBERTa-base~\\citep{liu2019roberta} (Section~\\ref{sec:cross_encoder}).\nWe also compare TF-IDF LR, LLM-as-judge (Appendix~\\ref{sec:llm_judge}), and Llama Guard~3~8B~\\citep{metallamaguard3_2024} (zero-shot; evaluates last-response safety given conversation context), which detects only 1.8\\% of multi-turn jailbreaks (Table~\\ref{tab:evidence_hierarchy}).\n\n\\paragraph{Metrics.}\nF1 for binary jailbreak detection at prefix $K$=2 and full conversations (full per-$K$ results in Appendix~\\ref{sec:full_prefix}).\nOOD results report 3-seed mean$\\pm$std (seeds 42, 123, 456) for all variants; TOST equivalence tests for 9-class vs.\\ scrambled use 5 seeds (42, 123, 456, 789, 1000).\nFor key pairwise comparisons, we report BCa bootstrap 95\\% confidence intervals (10,000 resamples over individual predictions pooled across 3 seeds; this assumes cross-seed independence and is exploratory) and paired permutation tests (10,000 permutations). Seed-level F1 values (Table~\\ref{tab:seed_level}, Appendix~\\ref{app:seed_level}) confirm all pairwise conclusions hold at every seed.\nUnless otherwise noted, all standard deviations are computed over 3 random seeds using the population formula ($n$ divisor); Bessel-corrected estimates ($n{-}1$ divisor) are $\\approx$22\\% larger but do not alter any significance conclusions.\nStage-1: learning rate $2 \\times 10^{-5}$, batch 16, 4 epochs, MLM masking 15\\%.\nStage-2: two-layer MLP, 20 epochs.\n\n%----------------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Cross-Attack Generalization}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cross-attack generalization (F1) at $K$=2 and full conversation length. IID: Crescendo; OOD: Deceptive Delight (DD). OOD F1 reflects attack-side generalization; benign samples are IID from training distribution. 3-seed mean$\\pm$std where noted; full per-$K$ in Appendix~\\ref{sec:full_prefix}.}\n\\label{tab:main_results}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc|cc@{}}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{2}{c|}{\\textit{IID (Cresc.)}} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textit{OOD (DD)}} \\\\\n\\textbf{Method} & $K$=2 & Full & $K$=2 & Full \\\\\n\\midrule\n9-class & \\textbf{1.000$\\pm$.000} & \\textbf{1.000$\\pm$.000} & .981$\\pm$.013 & \\textbf{.994$\\pm$.005} \\\\\nMLM-only$^{\\ddagger}$ & .974 & 1.000 & \\textbf{.993$\\pm$.009} & .914$\\pm$.115 \\\\\nVanilla & .978$\\pm$.016 & 1.000$\\pm$.000 & .479$\\pm$.038 & .312$\\pm$.026 \\\\\nTF-IDF LR$^{\\S}$ & 1.000 & 1.000 & 1.000 & .961 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\vspace{-2pt}\n{\\footnotesize $^{\\ddagger}$MLM-only IID reports single seed (42); OOD reports 3-seed. $^{\\S}$TF-IDF is deterministic (no std).}\n\\end{table}\n\nVanilla encoders collapse under cross-attack transfer; domain-adapted variants recover near-perfect detection (Table~\\ref{tab:main_results}, Figure~\\ref{fig:cross_attack_bar}).\nDD exhibits high lexical separability (TF-IDF F1\\,=\\,0.961); ActorAttack, where TF-IDF collapses to 0.261, serves as the primary stress test for beyond-lexical generalization.\nOn OOD DD, vanilla DeBERTa collapses to F1 = 0.312$\\pm$0.026, worsening with more turns (0.628 at $K$=1 to 0.312 at Full), consistent with attack turns occupying ``benign'' embedding regions~\\citep{bullwinkel2025repeng}.\nMLM achieves mean F1 $\\geq$ 0.97 at all prefixes $K \\leq 5$ without any classification labels; domain exposure alone is sufficient.\nAt full conversation length, MLM drops to 0.914$\\pm$0.115 (seed 456 recall = 0.64), while the 9-class model maintains 0.994$\\pm$0.005.\nTF-IDF LR reaches F1 = 0.961 on full DD; DD is fully solvable by lexical features.\nOn structurally complex ActorAttack, TF-IDF collapses to F1 = 0.261 (precision = 1.0, recall = 0.15), while 9-class maintains 0.972$\\pm$0.026: a 71\\,pp gap. Lexical methods cannot capture multi-persona role-playing attack structure. This establishes a ``difficulty threshold'' for lexical methods parallel to that of vanilla encoders: both succeed on DD and fail on ActorAttack (Section~\\ref{sec:limitations}).\nSeed-level F1 values confirm all pairwise conclusions hold at every seed (Appendix~\\ref{app:seed_level}); pooled BCa CIs provide exploratory confirmation (9-class DD Full [0.943, 1.000] vs.\\ vanilla [0.202, 0.341]; all permutation $p < 0.0001$). FITD serves as a near-ceiling baseline where all variants achieve F1\\,$\\geq$\\,0.990, providing limited discriminative power.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig2_cross_attack_bar.pdf}\n\\caption{Cross-attack generalization (full conversation F1). DisPeD (9-class) maintains near-perfect detection across all OOD families. On ActorAttack, both vanilla DeBERTa and TF-IDF collapse ($\\leq$0.26), exposing a difficulty threshold that lexical and non-adapted methods cannot cross.}\n\\label{fig:cross_attack_bar}\n\\end{figure}\n\nPrefix-length analysis (Appendix Table~\\ref{tab:full_prefix}) shows vanilla F1 drops from 0.628 at $K$=1 to 0.312 at full length. MLM achieves strong early detection ($K$=2: 0.993) but is unstable at full length (0.914$\\pm$0.115); the 9-class model maintains 0.972--0.994 across all prefixes, demonstrating that classification supervision stabilizes against distributional drift.\n\n%----------------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Decomposing the Detection Mechanism}\n\\label{sec:decomposition}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Evidence hierarchy on OOD DD and ActorAttack (AA) at full conversation length (3-seed mean$\\pm$std where available). Bold indicates best per column. OOD F1 reflects attack-side generalization only; benign samples are IID from training distribution (benign-side shift evaluated in Section~\\ref{sec:discussion}). Full per-prefix results in Appendix Tables~\\ref{tab:full_prefix} and~\\ref{tab:actorattack_full}.}\n\\label{tab:evidence_hierarchy}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Variant} & DD-Full & AA-Full & DD-$K$=2 \\\\\n\\midrule\n9-class (full model) & .994$\\pm$.005 & .972$\\pm$.026 & .981$\\pm$.013 \\\\\nBinary (2-class) & \\textbf{1.000$\\pm$.000} & \\textbf{.990$\\pm$.008} & .994$\\pm$.009 \\\\\nScrambled labels & .994$\\pm$.005 & .951$\\pm$.043 & .981$\\pm$.026 \\\\\nMLM-only$^{\\ddagger}$ (no labels) & .914$\\pm$.115 & .893$\\pm$.080 & .993$\\pm$.009 \\\\\n9-class + mean pool$^{*}$ & .991$\\pm$.013 & .962$\\pm$.015 & \\textbf{1.000} \\\\\n\\midrule\nTopic classif.\\ (5-class) & .541$\\pm$.207 & .625$\\pm$.141 & .593$\\pm$.166 \\\\\nWikipedia MLM & .904$\\pm$.115 & .686$\\pm$.297 & .911$\\pm$.073 \\\\\nVanilla DeBERTa & .312$\\pm$.026 & .258$\\pm$.000 & .479$\\pm$.038 \\\\\nTF-IDF LR$^{\\dagger}$ & .961 & .261 & \\textbf{1.000} \\\\\nLlama Guard~3$^{\\P}$ & .025 & .000 & -- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\vspace{-2pt}\n{\\footnotesize $^{*}$9-class encoder with mean pooling replacing BiGRU. $^{\\dagger}$Deterministic (no std). $^{\\ddagger}$MLM-only IID (Table~\\ref{tab:main_results}) is single-seed (42); all OOD values are 3-seed. $^{\\P}$Zero-shot; no domain training.}\n\\end{table}\n\nA clear \\emph{evidence hierarchy} emerges across seven variants (Table~\\ref{tab:evidence_hierarchy}), ordered by OOD detection at full conversation length on DD:\n\n\\vspace{2pt}\n\\noindent\\hspace{0.5em}{\\small vanilla (0.312$\\pm$0.026) $<$ topic (0.541$\\pm$0.207) $\\ll$ wiki-MLM (0.904$\\pm$0.115) $\\approx$ jb-MLM (0.914$\\pm$0.115) $<$ scrambled (0.994$\\pm$0.005) $=$ 9-class (0.994$\\pm$0.005) $\\approx$ binary (1.000$\\pm$0.000)}\n\\vspace{2pt}\n\n\\paragraph{Domain exposure is the primary mechanism.}\nMLM bridges $\\sim$60\\,pp from vanilla (0.312$\\pm$0.026 $\\to$ 0.914$\\pm$0.115) without labels; this supports the DAPT interpretation~\\citep{gururangan2020dontstop}. TF-IDF collapse on ActorAttack (0.261 vs.\\ 9-class 0.972) confirms that domain-adapted representations capture features beyond lexical overlap.\nAll three classification-supervised variants converge: any deterministic binary partition suffices for near-perfect detection (F1 = 0.994--1.000 on DD). Extended to $n$\\,=\\,10 seeds (each using an independent random permutation), TOST (margin = 5\\,pp) confirms equivalence on DD ($p < 10^{-8}$, $d$\\,=\\,$-$0.14; Table~\\ref{tab:tost_sensitivity}) and FITD ($p < 10^{-8}$, $d \\approx 0$), with a complexity-dependent boundary: template attacks show full equivalence ($d < 0.1$), while structurally complex ActorAttack retains moderate label sensitivity ($d$\\,=\\,0.44, $p$\\,=\\,0.183; 9-class 0.979 vs.\\ scrambled 0.949). Label granularity (binary vs.\\ 9-class) is a non-factor across all families.\nWe observed 4--20$\\times$ lower cross-seed variance for classification supervision over MLM on synthetic OOD (std 0.005 vs.\\ 0.115 on DD; observed across 3 seeds, with consistent directionality across 5 seeds on a second GPU, $n$\\,=\\,5, 4.6$\\times$; formal variance ratio tests are not applicable at these sample sizes; cross-seed ensemble yields marginal additional gains, Appendix~\\ref{app:ensemble}). This stability benefit does not transfer to human-authored data, where classification-supervised variants exhibit the highest variance and pure MLM the lowest (Section~\\ref{sec:human_data}).\nAppendix~\\ref{app:tsne} provides t-SNE visualizations confirming that domain-adapted variants produce tighter, more separable embedding clusters than the vanilla baseline.\n\n\\paragraph{Domain specificity is marginal at full length but meaningful for early detection.}\nWikipedia MLM (0.904$\\pm$0.115) nearly matches jailbreak-domain MLM at full length (1\\,pp gap), though jailbreak-domain text provides an 8\\,pp advantage at early prefixes ($K$=2: 0.993 vs.\\ 0.911).\nPerturbation analysis (Section~\\ref{sec:perturbation}) reveals a sharper domain specificity effect: Wikipedia MLM degrades by $\\sim$12\\,pp under adversarial paraphrasing while jailbreak-domain MLM degrades by only 2.6\\,pp.\n\n\\paragraph{Misaligned supervision destabilizes generalization.}\nTopic classification yields DD-Full F1 = 0.541$\\pm$0.207; seed 123 falls \\emph{below} vanilla (0.252 vs.\\ 0.312). Despite using the same jailbreak-domain training data, topic classification performs worse than Wikipedia MLM (0.904$\\pm$0.115), which uses out-of-domain text with no labels. A misaligned objective forces the encoder to allocate capacity to irrelevant features and actively destabilizes representations that MLM would preserve.\n\n\\paragraph{Evidence hierarchy on structurally complex attacks.}\nActorAttack~\\citep{ren2024actorattack} uses multi-persona steering across longer conversation structures and provides a more challenging OOD benchmark than DD.\nThe evidence hierarchy at full conversation length is:\n\n\\vspace{2pt}\n\\noindent\\hspace{0.5em}{\\small TF-IDF (0.261) $\\approx$ vanilla (0.258$\\pm$0.000) $<$ topic (0.625$\\pm$0.141) $<$ wiki-MLM (0.686$\\pm$0.297) $<$ jb-MLM (0.893$\\pm$0.080) $<$ scrambled (0.951$\\pm$0.043) $\\approx$ 9-class (0.972$\\pm$0.026) $\\approx$ binary (0.990$\\pm$0.008)}\n\\vspace{2pt}\n\nTF-IDF collapses from 0.961 (DD) to 0.261 (ActorAttack, precision = 1.0, recall = 0.15). This gap shows that domain-adapted representations capture features beyond bag-of-words.\nClassification-supervised variants cluster tightly (0.951--0.990, max gap 3.9\\,pp at $n$\\,=\\,3), though 10-seed TOST resolves the 9-class vs.\\ scrambled gap as a moderate effect ($d$\\,=\\,0.44). DAPT provides $\\sim$71\\,pp over both vanilla (0.258) and TF-IDF (0.261). Full per-prefix results and per-prefix error analysis in Appendix~\\ref{sec:actorattack_full} and~\\ref{app:aa_error}.\n\n\\paragraph{Temporal modeling.}\nMean pooling matches BiGRU on both DD and ActorAttack (F1 gap $\\leq$ 1\\,pp, overlapping CIs; Appendix~\\ref{app:temporal}).\nTurn-order shuffling produces negligible change ($|\\Delta| < 1$\\,pp) across both DeBERTa-v3-base 9-class (DD $\\Delta$\\,=\\,0.000, AA $\\Delta$\\,=\\,$-$0.009, IID $\\Delta$\\,=\\,0.000) and RoBERTa-large 9-class (DD $\\Delta$\\,=\\,+0.001, AA $\\Delta$\\,=\\,$-$0.007, IID $\\Delta$\\,=\\,0.000), confirming that detection relies on per-turn representations rather than sequential patterns.\n\n\\paragraph{Summary.}\nOn synthetic multi-turn data, domain exposure is the primary mechanism; any deterministic partition suffices for template attacks ($d < 0.1$ on DD/FITD) with a complexity-dependent boundary ($d$\\,=\\,0.44 on ActorAttack); label granularity, temporal modeling, and domain specificity are non-factors. Classification supervision is associated with observed variance reduction of 4--20$\\times$ across 3 seeds (cross-hardware $n{=}5$: 4.6$\\times$) without changing the detection ceiling. Detection is stable across conversation lengths (Appendix~\\ref{app:length}), near-saturates at 50 conversations (Appendix~\\ref{app:data_efficiency}), and is insensitive to auxiliary loss weight (Appendix~\\ref{app:lambda}). Benign conversations average 12--20 turns while jailbreak conversations average 3--10 turns; Appendix~\\ref{app:length} reports length controls: truncation preserves 0\\% FPR, single-turn detection achieves F1\\,=\\,0.773, and padding degrades ActorAttack by $-$57.9\\,pp.\n\n%----------------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Cross-Architecture Observation}\n\\label{sec:cross_encoder}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cross-architecture comparison on OOD Full (3-seed mean$\\pm$std). DD = Deceptive Delight, AA = ActorAttack. Bold indicates best per encoder group.}\n\\label{tab:cross_encoder}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Variant} & DD & AA \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{3}{l}{\\textit{DeBERTa-v3-base (184M)}} \\\\[2pt]\nVanilla & .312$\\pm$.026 & .258$\\pm$.000 \\\\\nMLM & .914$\\pm$.115 & .893$\\pm$.080 \\\\\n9-class & \\textbf{.994$\\pm$.005} & \\textbf{.972$\\pm$.026} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{3}{l}{\\textit{RoBERTa-base (125M)}} \\\\[2pt]\nVanilla & .981$\\pm$.026 & .466$\\pm$.163 \\\\\nMLM & .987$\\pm$.018 & .730$\\pm$.201 \\\\\n9-class & \\textbf{.994$\\pm$.005} & \\textbf{.939$\\pm$.046} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{3}{l}{\\textit{RoBERTa-large (355M)}} \\\\[2pt]\nVanilla & .927$\\pm$.036 & \\textbf{1.000$\\pm$.000} \\\\\nMLM & .884$\\pm$.065 & .874$\\pm$.014 \\\\\n9-class & \\textbf{.987$\\pm$.004} & .946$\\pm$.057 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nWe replicate vanilla, MLM, and 9-class on RoBERTa~\\citep{liu2019roberta} (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_encoder}); since DeBERTa-v3 and RoBERTa differ in architecture and pretraining, not only scale, all cross-encoder comparisons reflect both factors.\nOn DD, RoBERTa-base vanilla already achieves 0.981$\\pm$0.026 (vs.\\ DeBERTa 0.312$\\pm$0.026), so DAPT adds only 1.3\\,pp. ActorAttack exposes this as DD's simplicity: RoBERTa-base vanilla collapses to 0.466$\\pm$0.163, a 51.5\\,pp drop, while domain-adapted variants recover (9-class: 0.939$\\pm$0.046).\n\nRoBERTa-large (355M) reverses the pattern on ActorAttack: vanilla F1\\,=\\,1.000 vs.\\ 9-class 0.946, suggesting DAPT may be unnecessary or mildly harmful for strong pretrained encoders. Smaller models require DAPT to bridge 47--71\\,pp. All continued pretraining reduces ActorAttack performance for the largest encoder via representation drift (Table~\\ref{tab:roberta_large_lr}), though classification DAPT partially compensates on DD (+6\\,pp).\n\nThree encoder scales reveal a scale-dependent \\emph{difficulty threshold}: DeBERTa-v3-base requires adaptation for all attacks, RoBERTa-base only for complex ones, RoBERTa-large for none. A BERT-base-uncased proxy (110M, matching DeepContext) confirms cross-attack generalization is driven by DAPT, not architecture (Appendix~\\ref{app:bert_proxy}). CKA analysis~\\citep{kornblith2019cka} confirms DAPT reshapes representations substantially (Figure~\\ref{fig:cka_heatmap}; Appendix~\\ref{sec:cka_heatmap}).\n\n%----------------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Mixed-LLM Training}\n\nAll preceding experiments use single-source (Qwen3-8B) data. To test whether the evidence hierarchy transfers when training data mixes LLM sources, we train on 250 Qwen + 250 Llama-3.1-8B conversations (Table~\\ref{tab:mixed_llm}).\nClassification-based DAPT is the only variant that substantially outperforms vanilla: 9-class achieves DD OOD F1\\,=\\,0.818$\\pm$0.096 (+53\\,pp over vanilla 0.291), while MLM drops to vanilla level (0.289$\\pm$0.101). ActorAttack remains challenging in this exploratory cross-LLM condition: 9-class AA F1\\,=\\,0.504$\\pm$0.107 falls below a trivial all-benign baseline ($\\sim$0.808, the benign class proportion), indicating that 125 per-source jailbreak conversations are insufficient for structurally complex attacks under LLM-source diversity. Benign-only adaptation on Llama-origin data collapses Llama jailbreak detection (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}), suggesting LLM-specific style features confound the adaptation. Full results in Appendix~\\ref{app:mixed_llm}.\n\n%----------------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Human Data Validation}\n\\label{sec:human_data}\n\nSince both training and OOD evaluation data are Qwen3-8B-generated, we validate on ToxicChat~\\citep{lin2023toxicchat} (204 human-written jailbreak attempts + 204 benign conversations from real LMSYS interactions).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Human data validation on ToxicChat~\\citep{lin2023toxicchat} (macro F1, 3-seed mean$\\pm$std). Bold indicates best. Single-turn data from real LMSYS user interactions, independent of Qwen3-8B.}\n\\label{tab:human_data}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lc@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Variant} & Macro F1 \\\\\n\\midrule\nJB-MLM & \\textbf{.837$\\pm$.013} \\\\\nWiki-MLM & .791$\\pm$.093 \\\\\nBinary & .705$\\pm$.072 \\\\\nScrambled & .616$\\pm$.056 \\\\\n9-class & .596$\\pm$.087 \\\\\nTopic & .496$\\pm$.042 \\\\\n\\midrule\nVanilla & .413$\\pm$.021 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nAll DAPT variants exceed vanilla by 18--42\\,pp (Table~\\ref{tab:human_data}), confirming transfer to human-written attacks. However, the evidence hierarchy reverses: JB-MLM leads (0.837$\\pm$0.013) while 9-class ranks lower (0.596$\\pm$0.087; $p < 0.001$). This reversal---analyzed in Section~\\ref{sec:discussion}---reveals that classification DAPT's advantage is condition-specific to multi-turn persuasion attacks.\n\n%----------------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Perturbation Analysis}\n\\label{sec:perturbation}\n\nUnder adversarial paraphrasing (BERTScore 0.92), classification-supervised variants achieve zero degradation on IID and OOD DD (Table~\\ref{tab:ood_perturbation}) while non-adapted models degrade by $\\sim$12\\,pp ($p < 0.001$); on ActorAttack, DAPT variants degrade ($\\Delta$\\,=\\,$-$20.4\\,pp) but maintain 52\\,pp over vanilla (Appendix~\\ref{sec:perturbation_full}).\n\nStrategy trajectory analysis ($\\kappa$=0.78 on known attacks, $\\kappa$=0.126 on OOD; Appendix~\\ref{sec:strategy_full}) confirms the taxonomy's value as ablation infrastructure rather than a detection mechanism.\n",

OOD reports 3-seed. $^{\\S}$TF-IDF is deterministic (no std).}\n\\end{table}\n\nVanilla encoders collapse under cross-attack transfer; domain-adapted variants recover near-perfect detection (Table~\\ref{tab:main_results}, Figure~\\ref{fig:cross_attack_bar}).\nDD exhibits high lexical separability (TF-IDF F1\\,=\\,0.961); ActorAttack, where TF-IDF collapses to 0.261, serves as the primary stress test for beyond-lexical generalization.\nOn OOD DD, vanilla DeBERTa collapses to F1 = 0.312$\\pm$0.026, worsening with more turns (0.628 at $K$=1 to 0.312 at Full), consistent with attack turns occupying ``benign'' embedding regions~\\citep{bullwinkel2025repeng}.\nMLM achieves mean F1 $\\geq$ 0.97 at all prefixes $K \\leq 5$ without any classification labels; domain exposure alone is sufficient.\nAt full conversation length, MLM drops to 0.914$\\pm$0.115 (seed 456 recall = 0.64), while the 9-class model maintains 0.994$\\pm$0.005.\nTF-IDF LR reaches F1 = 0.961 on full DD; DD is fully solvable by lexical features.\nOn structurally complex ActorAttack, TF-IDF collapses to F1 = 0.261 (precision = 1.0, recall = 0.15), while 9-class maintains 0.972$\\pm$0.026: a 71\\,pp gap. Lexical methods cannot capture multi-persona role-playing attack structure. This establishes a ``difficulty threshold'' for lexical methods parallel to that of vanilla encoders: both succeed on DD and fail on ActorAttack (Section~\\ref{sec:limitations}).\nSeed-level F1 values confirm all pairwise conclusions hold at every seed (Appendix~\\ref{app:seed_level}); pooled BCa CIs provide exploratory confirmation (9-class DD Full [0.943, 1.000] vs.\\ vanilla [0.202, 0.341]; all permutation $p < 0.0001$). FITD serves as a near-ceiling baseline where all variants achieve F1\\,$\\geq$\\,0.990, providing limited discriminative power.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig2_cross_attack_bar.pdf}\n\\caption{Cross-attack generalization (full conversation F1). MLM achieves strong early detection ($K$=2: 0.993) but is unstable at full length (0.914$\\pm$0.115); the 9-class model maintains 0.972--0.994 across all prefixes, demonstrating that classification supervision stabilizes against distributional drift.\n\n%----------------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Decomposing the Detection Mechanism}\n\\label{sec:decomposition}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Evidence hierarchy on OOD DD and ActorAttack (AA) at full conversation length (3-seed mean$\\pm$std where available). Bold indicates best per column. OOD F1 reflects attack-side generalization only; benign samples are IID from training distribution (benign-side shift evaluated in Section~\\ref{sec:discussion}). all OOD values are 3-seed. $^{\\P}$Zero-shot; no domain training.}\n\\end{table}\n\nA clear \\emph{evidence hierarchy} emerges across seven variants (Table~\\ref{tab:evidence_hierarchy}), ordered by OOD detection at full conversation length on DD:\n\n\\vspace{2pt}\n\\noindent\\hspace{0.5em}{\\small vanilla (0.312$\\pm$0.026) $<$ topic (0.541$\\pm$0.207) $\\ll$ wiki-MLM (0.904$\\pm$0.115) $\\approx$ jb-MLM (0.914$\\pm$0.115) $<$ scrambled (0.994$\\pm$0.005) $=$ 9-class (0.994$\\pm$0.005) $\\approx$ binary (1.000$\\pm$0.000)}\n\\vspace{2pt}\n\n\\paragraph{Domain exposure is the primary mechanism.}\nMLM bridges $\\sim$60\\,pp from vanilla (0.312$\\pm$0.026 $\\to$ 0.914$\\pm$0.115) without labels; this supports the DAPT interpretation~\\citep{gururangan2020dontstop}. TF-IDF collapse on ActorAttack (0.261 vs.\\ 9-class 0.972) confirms that domain-adapted representations capture features beyond lexical overlap.\nAll three classification-supervised variants converge: any deterministic binary partition suffices for near-perfect detection (F1 = 0.994--1.000 on DD). Extended to $n$\\,=\\,10 seeds (each using an independent random permutation), TOST (margin = 5\\,pp) confirms equivalence on DD ($p < 10^{-8}$, $d$\\,=\\,$-$0.14; Table~\\ref{tab:tost_sensitivity}) and FITD ($p < 10^{-8}$, $d \\approx 0$), with a complexity-dependent boundary: template attacks show full equivalence ($d < 0.1$), while structurally complex ActorAttack retains moderate label sensitivity ($d$\\,=\\,0.44, $p$\\,=\\,0.183; 9-class 0.979 vs.\\ scrambled 0.949). Label granularity (binary vs.\\ 9-class) is a non-factor across all families.\nWe observed 4--20$\\times$ lower cross-seed variance for classification supervision over MLM on synthetic OOD (std 0.005 vs.\\ 0.115 on DD; observed across 3 seeds, with consistent directionality across 5 seeds on a second GPU, $n$\\,=\\,5, 4.6$\\times$; formal variance ratio tests are not applicable at these sample sizes; cross-seed ensemble yields marginal additional gains, Appendix~\\ref{app:ensemble}). This stability benefit does not transfer to human-authored data, where classification-supervised variants exhibit the highest variance and pure MLM the lowest (Section~\\ref{sec:human_data}).\nAppendix~\\ref{app:tsne} provides t-SNE visualizations confirming that domain-adapted variants produce tighter, more separable embedding clusters than the vanilla baseline.\n\n\\paragraph{Domain specificity is marginal at full length but meaningful for early detection.}\nWikipedia MLM (0.904$\\pm$0.115) nearly matches jailbreak-domain MLM at full length (1\\,pp gap), though jailbreak-domain text provides an 8\\,pp advantage at early prefixes ($K$=2: 0.993 vs.\\ 0.911).\nPerturbation analysis (Section~\\ref{sec:perturbation}) reveals a sharper domain specificity effect: Wikipedia MLM degrades by $\\sim$12\\,pp under adversarial paraphrasing while jailbreak-domain MLM degrades by only 2.6\\,pp.\n\n\\paragraph{Misaligned supervision destabilizes generalization.}\nTopic classification yields DD-Full F1 = 0.541$\\pm$0.207; seed 123 falls \\emph{below} vanilla (0.252 vs.\\ 0.312). Despite using the same jailbreak-domain training data, topic classification performs worse than Wikipedia MLM (0.904$\\pm$0.115), which uses out-of-domain text with no labels. A misaligned objective forces the encoder to allocate capacity to irrelevant features and actively destabilizes representations that MLM would preserve.\n\n\\paragraph{Evidence hierarchy on structurally complex attacks.}\nActorAttack~\\citep{ren2024actorattack} uses multi-persona steering across longer conversation structures and provides a more challenging OOD benchmark than DD.\nThe evidence hierarchy at full conversation length is:\n\n\\vspace{2pt}\n\\noindent\\hspace{0.5em}{\\small TF-IDF (0.261) $\\approx$ vanilla (0.258$\\pm$0.000) $<$ topic (0.625$\\pm$0.141) $<$ wiki-MLM (0.686$\\pm$0.297) $<$ jb-MLM (0.893$\\pm$0.080) $<$ scrambled (0.951$\\pm$0.043) $\\approx$ 9-class (0.972$\\pm$0.026) $\\approx$ binary (0.990$\\pm$0.008)}\n\\vspace{2pt}\n\nTF-IDF collapses from 0.961 (DD) to 0.261 (ActorAttack, precision = 1.0, recall = 0.15). This gap shows that domain-adapted representations capture features beyond bag-of-words.\nClassification-supervised variants cluster tightly (0.951--0.990, max gap 3.9\\,pp at $n$\\,=\\,3), though 10-seed TOST resolves the 9-class vs.\\ scrambled gap as a moderate effect ($d$\\,=\\,0.44). DAPT provides $\\sim$71\\,pp over both vanilla (0.258) and TF-IDF (0.261). Full per-prefix results and per-prefix error analysis in Appendix~\\ref{sec:actorattack_full} and~\\ref{app:aa_error}.\n\n\\paragraph{Temporal modeling.}\nMean pooling matches BiGRU on both DD and ActorAttack (F1 gap $\\leq$ 1\\,pp, overlapping CIs; Appendix~\\ref{app:temporal}).\nTurn-order shuffling produces negligible change ($|\\Delta| < 1$\\,pp) across both DeBERTa-v3-base 9-class (DD $\\Delta$\\,=\\,0.000, AA $\\Delta$\\,=\\,$-$0.009, IID $\\Delta$\\,=\\,0.000) and RoBERTa-large 9-class (DD $\\Delta$\\,=\\,+0.001, AA $\\Delta$\\,=\\,$-$0.007, IID $\\Delta$\\,=\\,0.000), confirming that detection relies on per-turn representations rather than sequential patterns.\n\n\\paragraph{Summary.}\nOn synthetic multi-turn data, domain exposure is the primary mechanism; any deterministic partition suffices for template attacks ($d < 0.1$ on DD/FITD) with a complexity-dependent boundary ($d$\\,=\\,0.44 on ActorAttack); label granularity, temporal modeling, and domain specificity are non-factors. Classification supervision is associated with observed variance reduction of 4--20$\\times$ across 3 seeds (cross-hardware $n{=}5$: 4.6$\\times$) without changing the detection ceiling. Detection is stable across conversation lengths (Appendix~\\ref{app:length}), near-saturates at 50 conversations (Appendix~\\ref{app:data_efficiency}), and is insensitive to auxiliary loss weight (Appendix~\\ref{app:lambda}). Benign conversations average 12--20 turns while jailbreak conversations average 3--10 turns; Appendix~\\ref{app:length} reports length controls: truncation preserves 0\\% FPR, single-turn detection achieves F1\\,=\\,0.773, and padding degrades ActorAttack by $-$57.9\\,pp.\n\n%----------------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Cross-Architecture Observation}\n\\label{sec:cross_encoder}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cross-architecture comparison on OOD Full (3-seed mean$\\pm$std). DD = Deceptive Delight, AA = ActorAttack. Bold indicates best per encoder group.}\n\\label{tab:cross_encoder}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Variant} & DD & AA \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{3}{l}{\\textit{DeBERTa-v3-base (184M)}} \\\\[2pt]\nVanilla & .312$\\pm$.026 & .258$\\pm$.000 \\\\\nMLM & .914$\\pm$.115 & .893$\\pm$.080 \\\\\n9-class & \\textbf{.994$\\pm$.005} & \\textbf{.972$\\pm$.026} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{3}{l}{\\textit{RoBERTa-base (125M)}} \\\\[2pt]\nVanilla & .981$\\pm$.026 & .466$\\pm$.163 \\\\\nMLM & .987$\\pm$.018 & .730$\\pm$.201 \\\\\n9-class & \\textbf{.994$\\pm$.005} & \\textbf{.939$\\pm$.046} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{3}{l}{\\textit{RoBERTa-large (355M)}} \\\\[2pt]\nVanilla & .927$\\pm$.036 & \\textbf{1.000$\\pm$.000} \\\\\nMLM & .884$\\pm$.065 & .874$\\pm$.014 \\\\\n9-class & \\textbf{.987$\\pm$.004} & .946$\\pm$.057 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nWe replicate vanilla, MLM, and 9-class on RoBERTa~\\citep{liu2019roberta} (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_encoder}); since DeBERTa-v3 and RoBERTa differ in architecture and pretraining, not only scale, all cross-encoder comparisons reflect both factors.\nOn DD, RoBERTa-base vanilla already achieves 0.981$\\pm$0.026 (vs.\\ DeBERTa 0.312$\\pm$0.026), so DAPT adds only 1.3\\,pp. ActorAttack exposes this as DD's simplicity: RoBERTa-base vanilla collapses to 0.466$\\pm$0.163, a 51.5\\,pp drop, while domain-adapted variants recover (9-class: 0.939$\\pm$0.046).\n\nRoBERTa-large (355M) reverses the pattern on ActorAttack: vanilla F1\\,=\\,1.000 vs.\\ 9-class 0.946, suggesting DAPT may be unnecessary or mildly harmful for strong pretrained encoders. Smaller models require DAPT to bridge 47--71\\,pp. All continued pretraining reduces ActorAttack performance for the largest encoder via representation drift (Table~\\ref{tab:roberta_large_lr}), though classification DAPT partially compensates on DD (+6\\,pp).\n\nThree encoder scales reveal a scale-dependent \\emph{difficulty threshold}: DeBERTa-v3-base requires adaptation for all attacks, RoBERTa-base only for complex ones, RoBERTa-large for none. A BERT-base-uncased proxy (110M, matching DeepContext) confirms cross-attack generalization is driven by DAPT, not architecture (Appendix~\\ref{app:bert_proxy}). CKA analysis~\\citep{kornblith2019cka} confirms DAPT reshapes representations substantially (Figure~\\ref{fig:cka_heatmap}; Appendix~\\ref{sec:cka_heatmap}).\n\n%----------------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Mixed-LLM Training}\n\nAll preceding experiments use single-source (Qwen3-8B) data. To test whether the evidence hierarchy transfers when training data mixes LLM sources, we train on 250 Qwen + 250 Llama-3.1-8B conversations (Table~\\ref{tab:mixed_llm}).\nIn this exploratory cross-LLM condition, 9-class achieves DD OOD F1\\,=\\,0.818$\\pm$0.096 (+53\\,pp over vanilla), while MLM drops to vanilla level (0.289). AA F1\\,=\\,0.504$\\pm$0.107 falls below the trivial all-benign baseline ($\\sim$0.808), and benign-only adaptation on Llama-origin data collapses Llama jailbreak detection (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}), suggesting LLM-specific style confounds. Full results in Appendix~\\ref{app:mixed_llm}.\n\n%----------------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Human Data Validation}\n\\label{sec:human_data}\n\nSince both training and OOD evaluation data are Qwen3-8B-generated, we validate on ToxicChat~\\citep{lin2023toxicchat} (204 human-written jailbreak attempts + 204 benign conversations from real LMSYS interactions).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Human data validation on ToxicChat~\\citep{lin2023toxicchat} (macro F1, 3-seed mean$\\pm$std). Bold indicates best. Single-turn data from real LMSYS user interactions, independent of Qwen3-8B.}\n\\label{tab:human_data}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lc@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Variant} & Macro F1 \\\\\n\\midrule\nJB-MLM & \\textbf{.837$\\pm$.013} \\\\\nWiki-MLM & .791$\\pm$.093 \\\\\nBinary & .705$\\pm$.072 \\\\\nScrambled & .616$\\pm$.056 \\\\\n9-class & .596$\\pm$.087 \\\\\nTopic & .496$\\pm$.042 \\\\\n\\midrule\nVanilla & .413$\\pm$.021 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nAll DAPT variants exceed vanilla by 18--42\\,pp (Table~\\ref{tab:human_data}), confirming transfer to human-written attacks. However, the evidence hierarchy reverses: JB-MLM leads (0.837$\\pm$0.013) while 9-class ranks lower (0.596$\\pm$0.087; $p < 0.001$). This reversal---analyzed in Section~\\ref{sec:discussion}---reveals that classification DAPT's advantage is condition-specific to multi-turn persuasion attacks.\n\n%----------------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Perturbation Analysis}\n\\label{sec:perturbation}\n\nUnder adversarial paraphrasing (BERTScore 0.92), classification-supervised variants achieve zero degradation on IID and OOD DD (Table~\\ref{tab:ood_perturbation}) while non-adapted models degrade by $\\sim$12\\,pp ($p < 0.001$); on ActorAttack, DAPT variants degrade ($\\Delta$\\,=\\,$-$20.4\\,pp) but maintain 52\\,pp over vanilla (Appendix~\\ref{sec:perturbation_full}).\n\nStrategy trajectory analysis ($\\kappa$=0.78 on known attacks, $\\kappa$=0.126 on OOD; Appendix~\\ref{sec:strategy_full}) confirms the taxonomy's value as ablation infrastructure rather than a detection mechanism.\n",

    "originalFile": "% related_work.tex — D048 deep writing optimization\n\n\\section{Related Work}\n\n\\paragraph{Multi-turn jailbreak attacks.}\nAll multi-turn jailbreak families share a persuasion substrate~\\citep{zeng2024pap,hagendorff2026reasoning,li2024mhj}: Crescendo~\\citep{russinovich2024crescendo}, FITD~\\citep{fitd2024}, DD~\\citep{deceptivedelight2024}, and ActorAttack~\\citep{ren2024actorattack} each operationalize it differently, while automated methods~\\citep{chao2023pair,mehrotra2024tap,perez2022redteaming} iteratively refine prompts and many-shot attacks~\\citep{anil2024manyshot} exploit long-context in-context learning. Benchmarks standardize evaluation at single-turn~\\citep{mazeika2024harmbench,chao2024jailbreakbench} and multi-turn~\\citep{wei2023jailbroken,jiang2024redqueen,song2026multibreak} scales. A detector must generalize across families, not merely succeed on its training distribution.\n\n\\paragraph{Multi-turn defenses and production safety.}\nNo existing defense evaluates cross-attack transfer. Conversation-level systems (DeepContext~\\citep{deepcontext2026}, BIID~\\citep{tong2025biid}, G-Guard~\\citep{gguard2025}, SafeDream~\\citep{safedream2026}, JailDAM~\\citep{nian2025jaildam}) and representation-level approaches~\\citep{lu2025xboundary,zhang2025jbshield,xie2024gradsafe,zou2024circuitbreakers,kulkarni2026latentadversarial,kadali2026trace,hua2026rcs,shen2025antidote,li2025repejailbreak,wang2026trajguard,templeton2024saemitigators} each evaluate on a single family; the latter require white-box access and can be brittle~\\citep{derya2026revisitingjbshield}. J-DAPT~\\citep{marchiori2025jdapt} applies CORAL-based domain adaptation with cross-modal fusion for multimodal robotic jailbreak detection; our work focuses on text-only multi-turn persuasion attacks and decomposes which adaptation factors drive cross-attack generalization through controlled ablation. Per-turn classifiers~\\citep{inan2023llamaguard,metallamaguard3_2024,zeng2024shieldgemma,han2024wildguard,anthropic2025constitutional} and single-turn defenses~\\citep{robey2024smoothllm,xu2024safedecoding,jain2023baseline,alon2023perplexity,phute2023selfdefense,hsu2024improved} miss multi-turn trajectories; \\citet{li2025m2s} propose converting multi-turn attacks to single-turn for existing guardrails, a complementary approach to our direct multi-turn detection. Our ablation contributes to filling this gap via decomposition across thirteen configurations under OOD evaluation.\n\n\\paragraph{Computational persuasion and discourse.}\nPersuasion and discourse frameworks~\\citep{austin1962speechacts,searle1969speechacts,mann1988rst,prasad2008pdtb,stolcke2000dialogueact} and technique-level classification~\\citep{cialdini2001influence,zeng2024pap,hagendorff2026reasoning,dasanmartino2019fine,dasanmartino2020semeval,wang2019persuasion} offer interpretable vocabulary for attacks, but whether such structure aids detection has not been tested. Our ablation shows the taxonomy enables characterization, but detection operates through domain-adapted embeddings regardless of label correctness.\n\n\\paragraph{Domain-adaptive pretraining.}\nContinued pretraining on domain text yields consistent gains~\\citep{gururangan2020dontstop,lee2020biobert,beltagy2019scibert,howard2018ulmfit}, but has not been disentangled for safety-critical generalization. Intermediate-task transfer~\\citep{phang2018stilts} and multi-task learning~\\citep{caruana1997multitask,liu2019mtdnn} confound domain exposure with supervised signal; our MLM control confirms pretraining alone closes the OOD gap. Dataset artifacts~\\citep{gururangan2018artifacts,niven2019probing} motivate TF-IDF, adversarial NLP~\\citep{jin2020textfooler,li2020bertattack,nasr2025attackermovessecond,andriushchenko2024adaptive} informs perturbation, and probing~\\citep{conneau2018probing,hewitt2019structural} underpins CKA analysis.\n",

    "originalFile": "% conclusion.tex — D073 Phase 2 revision (page-compressed)\n\n\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Confounds and transferability.}\nThree observations argue against a topic shortcut: unstable topic OOD (F1\\,=\\,0.541$\\pm$0.207), scrambled labels within 3.9\\,pp, and label-free MLM detection (F1\\,$\\geq$\\,0.97). ToxicChat (+18--42\\,pp; Table~\\ref{tab:human_data}) and cross-LLM experiments (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}) confirm transfer; mixed-LLM 9-class maintains DD F1\\,=\\,0.818$\\pm$0.096 while MLM collapses to vanilla level (Appendix~\\ref{app:mixed_llm}). Dataset composition---not sample count---is the primary determinant (Appendix~\\ref{app:scale_validation}). Prompt optimization improves LLM-as-judge OOD detection but does not close the gap with encoder-based methods (Appendix~\\ref{app:prompt_opt}).\n\n\\paragraph{Evidence for discriminative compression.}\nClassification DAPT compresses the feature space into few discriminative dimensions (participation ratio 3.7--9.4 vs.\\ 25--32 for MLM/vanilla) with 3--5 orders of magnitude higher separability (Fisher 2.6--19.7 vs.\\ $<$0.001; Appendix~\\ref{app:representation}). Scrambled labels produce comparable compression (Fisher\\,=\\,2.6), consistent with \\citet{zhang2021understanding}'s framework for how classification modifies representations, with two domain-specific findings: (i)~domain-relevant label partitioning is necessary (topic labels insufficient), and (ii)~compression localizes to 3 SVD dimensions in layers 8--12 (Fisher 15$\\times$ vanilla at peak; Appendix~\\ref{app:layerwise}), with early layers remaining label-independent as in \\citet{maennel2020random}. A discriminative probe confirms: 3 SVD components of 9-class layer-10 representations achieve perfect AA OOD detection (F1\\,=\\,1.000), while random projections degrade to 0.928 and MLM reaches only 0.636 with all 768 dimensions (Appendix~\\ref{app:discriminative_probe}). The complexity-dependent boundary persists (TOST $d < 0.1$ on DD/FITD; $d$\\,=\\,0.44 on AA). Frozen probing near-parity (AA gap 3.1\\,pp) confirms discriminative structure is established in Stage-1.\n\n\\paragraph{Condition-specific advantage.}\nThe evidence hierarchy is specific to multi-turn synthetic evaluation. On ToxicChat (single-turn human data), JB-MLM leads (0.837) while 9-class ranks lower (0.596): 77\\% from false positives and 23\\% from missed attacks lacking multi-turn signals. This reversal is not driven by multi-turn structure: truncating synthetic conversations to a single turn \\emph{widens} the 9-class advantage ($\\bar{\\Delta}$\\,=\\,+1.6\\,pp full $\\to$ +6.0\\,pp single-turn; Appendix~\\ref{app:truncated}), implicating distributional differences between synthetic and human text. Variance reverses (classification std\\,=\\,0.087 vs.\\ MLM\\,=\\,0.013); practitioners facing single-turn human data should consider MLM DAPT.\n\n\\paragraph{Two-axis generalization.}\nOOD F1 reflects attack-side generalization under IID benign conditions. The orthogonal challenge is benign-side shift: DAPT increases WildChat FPR from 48.1\\% to 68.1\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:wildchat_fpr}). Ten target-domain conversations reduce it to 0\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:benign_mixed}); OASST2 confirms the pattern (FPR 57.5\\%\\,$\\rightarrow$\\,1.0\\%$\\pm$0.5\\% at $N$\\,=\\,25; Appendix~\\ref{app:oasst2}).\n\n\\section{Conclusion}\n\nOn synthetic multi-turn data, thirteen encoder configurations decompose multi-turn jailbreak generalization into two orthogonal axes: attack-side (classification DAPT via discriminative compression; MLM collapses under mixed-LLM data) and benign-side (FPR 65--68\\%\\,$\\rightarrow$\\,0--1.5\\% via 10--25 conversations on two independent sources). Any deterministic partition suffices for template attacks ($d < 0.1$), with a complexity-dependent boundary on ActorAttack ($d$\\,=\\,0.44). With 24.3$\\times$ speedup over LLM-as-judge, these results suggest a preliminary two-step protocol without target-domain attack data; validation on naturalistic multi-turn attacks is needed.\n\n\\section{Limitations}\n\\label{sec:limitations}\n\nAll multi-turn findings derive from synthetic conversations generated by Qwen3-8B (350 training, 76--118 test per family); preliminary mixed-LLM training (Appendix~\\ref{app:mixed_llm}) suggests this limitation is addressable. Most pairwise comparisons rely on 3 seeds; key scrambled-vs.-correct comparisons are extended to 10 seeds (Appendix~\\ref{app:seed_level}). ToxicChat validates transfer to human-authored single-turn attacks (+18--42\\,pp over vanilla), but the evidence hierarchy reverses on this data (Section~\\ref{sec:discussion}), and no human-generated multi-turn jailbreak dataset exists.\n\nOOD results reflect attack-side generalization only; benign data is IID, so OOD F1 figures are upper bounds. On WildChat, 9-class FPR reaches 68.1\\%; 10 target-domain conversations eliminate it ($n$\\,=\\,49). OASST2 confirms the pattern but requires 25--50 conversations for comparable reduction (FPR\\,=\\,1.0\\%$\\pm$0.5\\% at $N$\\,=\\,25, 3 seeds; Appendix~\\ref{app:oasst2}); validation on additional platforms is needed. Residual calibration error (ECE\\,=\\,0.126--0.141; Appendix~\\ref{app:calibration}) suggests temperature scaling is needed for deployment.\n\nBenign-only adaptation addresses human-population distribution shift (WildChat, OASST2) but not cross-LLM distribution shift: adapting on Llama-origin benign conversations reduces FPR but collapses Llama jailbreak detection, as the model conflates LLM-specific style features with benign class membership (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}). Three encoders (125M--355M) differ in architecture and pretraining; same-architecture scaling is needed to isolate the capacity effect. Only persuasion-mediated multi-turn attacks fall within scope; single-turn token-level attacks~\\citep{zou2023gcg}, non-persuasive strategies, and non-text modalities are not addressed. Total pipeline cost is under 4 GPU-hours on a single A100.\n\n\\section*{Ethics Statement}\n\nThis work is defensive: it improves detection of multi-turn jailbreak attacks rather than developing new attacks. We acknowledge dual-use risk, as the persuasion taxonomy could inform adversarial actors. We mitigate this by using only synthetic conversations (no real harmful content) and withholding attack-generation prompts. Detection code, model weights, and evaluation data will be released under CC BY-NC 4.0. No human subjects were involved.\n",

```text
1	% conclusion.tex — D073 Phase 2 revision (page-compressed)
2	
3	\section{Discussion}
4	\label{sec:discussion}
5	
6	\paragraph{Confounds and transferability.}
7	Three observations argue against a topic shortcut: unstable topic OOD (F1\,=\,0.541$\pm$0.207), scrambled labels within 3.9\,pp, and label-free MLM detection (F1\,$\geq$\,0.97). ToxicChat (+18--42\,pp; Table~\ref{tab:human_data}) and cross-LLM experiments (Appendix~\ref{app:cross_llm}) confirm transfer; mixed-LLM 9-class maintains DD F1\,=\,0.818$\pm$0.096 while MLM collapses to vanilla level (Appendix~\ref{app:mixed_llm}). Dataset composition---not sample count---is the primary determinant (Appendix~\ref{app:scale_validation}). Prompt optimization improves LLM-as-judge OOD detection but does not close the gap with encoder-based methods (Appendix~\ref{app:prompt_opt}).
8	
9	\paragraph{Evidence for discriminative compression.}
10	Classification DAPT compresses the feature space into few discriminative dimensions (participation ratio 3.7--9.4 vs.\ 25--32 for MLM/vanilla) with 3--5 orders of magnitude higher separability (Fisher 2.6--19.7 vs.\ $<$0.001; Appendix~\ref{app:representation}). Scrambled labels produce comparable compression (Fisher\,=\,2.6), consistent with \citet{zhang2021understanding}'s framework for how classification modifies representations, with two domain-specific findings: (i)~domain-relevant label partitioning is necessary (topic labels insufficient), and (ii)~compression localizes to 3 SVD dimensions in layers 8--12 (Fisher 15$\times$ vanilla at peak; Appendix~\ref{app:layerwise}), with early layers remaining label-independent as in \citet{maennel2020random}. A discriminative probe confirms: 3 SVD components of 9-class layer-10 representations achieve perfect AA OOD detection (F1\,=\,1.000), while random projections degrade to 0.928 and MLM reaches only 0.636 with all 768 dimensions (Appendix~\ref{app:discriminative_probe}). The complexity-dependent boundary persists (TOST $d < 0.1$ on DD/FITD; $d$\,=\,0.44 on AA). Frozen probing near-parity (AA gap 3.1\,pp) confirms discriminative structure is established in Stage-1.
11	
12	\paragraph{Condition-specific advantage.}
13	The evidence hierarchy is specific to multi-turn synthetic evaluation. On ToxicChat (single-turn human data), JB-MLM leads (0.837) while 9-class ranks lower (0.596): 77\% from false positives and 23\% from missed attacks lacking multi-turn signals. This reversal is not driven by multi-turn structure: truncating synthetic conversations to a single turn \emph{widens} the 9-class advantage ($\bar{\Delta}$\,=\,+1.6\,pp full $\to$ +6.0\,pp single-turn; Appendix~\ref{app:truncated}), implicating distributional differences between synthetic and human text. Variance reverses (classification std\,=\,0.087 vs.\ MLM\,=\,0.013); practitioners facing single-turn human data should consider MLM DAPT.
14	
15	\paragraph{Two-axis generalization.}
16	OOD F1 reflects attack-side generalization under IID benign conditions. The orthogonal challenge is benign-side shift: DAPT increases WildChat FPR from 48.1\% to 68.1\% (Appendix~\ref{app:wildchat_fpr}). Ten target-domain conversations reduce it to 0\% (Appendix~\ref{app:benign_mixed}); OASST2 confirms the pattern (FPR 57.5\%\,$\rightarrow$\,1.0\%$\pm$0.5\% at $N$\,=\,25; Appendix~\ref{app:oasst2}).
17	
18	\section{Conclusion}
19	
20	On synthetic multi-turn data, thirteen encoder configurations decompose multi-turn jailbreak generalization into two orthogonal axes: attack-side (classification DAPT via discriminative compression; MLM collapses under mixed-LLM data) and benign-side (FPR 65--68\%\,$\rightarrow$\,0--1.5\% via 10--25 conversations on two independent sources). Any deterministic partition suffices for template attacks ($d < 0.1$), with a complexity-dependent boundary on ActorAttack ($d$\,=\,0.44). With 24.3$\times$ speedup over LLM-as-judge, these results suggest a preliminary two-step protocol without target-domain attack data.
```

    "newString": "On synthetic multi-turn data, thirteen encoder configurations decompose jailbreak generalization into attack-side (classification DAPT via discriminative compression; MLM collapses under mixed-LLM data) and benign-side (FPR 65--68\\%\\,$\\rightarrow$\\,0--1.5\\% via 10--25 conversations) axes. Any deterministic partition suffices for template attacks ($d < 0.1$), with a complexity-dependent boundary on ActorAttack ($d$\\,=\\,0.44). At 24.3$\\times$ speedup over LLM-as-judge, these suggest a preliminary two-step protocol.",
    "originalFile": "% conclusion.tex — D073 Phase 2 revision (page-compressed)\n\n\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Confounds and transferability.}\nThree observations argue against a topic shortcut: unstable topic OOD (F1\\,=\\,0.541$\\pm$0.207), scrambled labels within 3.9\\,pp, and label-free MLM detection (F1\\,$\\geq$\\,0.97). ToxicChat (+18--42\\,pp; Table~\\ref{tab:human_data}) and cross-LLM experiments (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}) confirm transfer; mixed-LLM 9-class maintains DD F1\\,=\\,0.818$\\pm$0.096 while MLM collapses to vanilla level (Appendix~\\ref{app:mixed_llm}). Dataset composition---not sample count---is the primary determinant (Appendix~\\ref{app:scale_validation}). Prompt optimization improves LLM-as-judge OOD detection but does not close the gap with encoder-based methods (Appendix~\\ref{app:prompt_opt}).\n\n\\paragraph{Evidence for discriminative compression.}\nClassification DAPT compresses the feature space into few discriminative dimensions (participation ratio 3.7--9.4 vs.\\ 25--32 for MLM/vanilla) with 3--5 orders of magnitude higher separability (Fisher 2.6--19.7 vs.\\ $<$0.001; Appendix~\\ref{app:representation}). Scrambled labels produce comparable compression (Fisher\\,=\\,2.6), consistent with \\citet{zhang2021understanding}'s framework for how classification modifies representations, with two domain-specific findings: (i)~domain-relevant label partitioning is necessary (topic labels insufficient), and (ii)~compression localizes to 3 SVD dimensions in layers 8--12 (Fisher 15$\\times$ vanilla at peak; Appendix~\\ref{app:layerwise}), with early layers remaining label-independent as in \\citet{maennel2020random}. A discriminative probe confirms: 3 SVD components of 9-class layer-10 representations achieve perfect AA OOD detection (F1\\,=\\,1.000), while random projections degrade to 0.928 and MLM reaches only 0.636 with all 768 dimensions (Appendix~\\ref{app:discriminative_probe}). The complexity-dependent boundary persists (TOST $d < 0.1$ on DD/FITD; $d$\\,=\\,0.44 on AA). Frozen probing near-parity (AA gap 3.1\\,pp) confirms discriminative structure is established in Stage-1.\n\n\\paragraph{Condition-specific advantage.}\nThe evidence hierarchy is specific to multi-turn synthetic evaluation. On ToxicChat (single-turn human data), JB-MLM leads (0.837) while 9-class ranks lower (0.596): 77\\% from false positives and 23\\% from missed attacks lacking multi-turn signals. This reversal is not driven by multi-turn structure: truncating synthetic conversations to a single turn \\emph{widens} the 9-class advantage ($\\bar{\\Delta}$\\,=\\,+1.6\\,pp full $\\to$ +6.0\\,pp single-turn; Appendix~\\ref{app:truncated}), implicating distributional differences between synthetic and human text. Variance reverses (classification std\\,=\\,0.087 vs.\\ MLM\\,=\\,0.013); practitioners facing single-turn human data should consider MLM DAPT.\n\n\\paragraph{Two-axis generalization.}\nOOD F1 reflects attack-side generalization under IID benign conditions. The orthogonal challenge is benign-side shift: DAPT increases WildChat FPR from 48.1\\% to 68.1\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:wildchat_fpr}). Ten target-domain conversations reduce it to 0\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:benign_mixed}); OASST2 confirms the pattern (FPR 57.5\\%\\,$\\rightarrow$\\,1.0\\%$\\pm$0.5\\% at $N$\\,=\\,25; Appendix~\\ref{app:oasst2}).\n\n\\section{Conclusion}\n\nOn synthetic multi-turn data, thirteen encoder configurations decompose multi-turn jailbreak generalization into two orthogonal axes: attack-side (classification DAPT via discriminative compression; MLM collapses under mixed-LLM data) and benign-side (FPR 65--68\\%\\,$\\rightarrow$\\,0--1.5\\% via 10--25 conversations on two independent sources). Any deterministic partition suffices for template attacks ($d < 0.1$), with a complexity-dependent boundary on ActorAttack ($d$\\,=\\,0.44). With 24.3$\\times$ speedup over LLM-as-judge, these results suggest a preliminary two-step protocol without target-domain attack data.\n\n\\section{Limitations}\n\\label{sec:limitations}\n\nAll multi-turn findings derive from synthetic conversations generated by Qwen3-8B (350 training, 76--118 test per family); preliminary mixed-LLM training (Appendix~\\ref{app:mixed_llm}) suggests this limitation is addressable. Most pairwise comparisons rely on 3 seeds; key scrambled-vs.-correct comparisons are extended to 10 seeds (Appendix~\\ref{app:seed_level}). ToxicChat validates transfer to human-authored single-turn attacks (+18--42\\,pp over vanilla), but the evidence hierarchy reverses on this data (Section~\\ref{sec:discussion}), and no human-generated multi-turn jailbreak dataset exists.\n\nOOD results reflect attack-side generalization only; benign data is IID, so OOD F1 figures are upper bounds. On WildChat, 9-class FPR reaches 68.1\\%; 10 target-domain conversations eliminate it ($n$\\,=\\,49). OASST2 confirms the pattern but requires 25--50 conversations for comparable reduction (FPR\\,=\\,1.0\\%$\\pm$0.5\\% at $N$\\,=\\,25, 3 seeds; Appendix~\\ref{app:oasst2}); validation on additional platforms is needed. Residual calibration error (ECE\\,=\\,0.126--0.141; Appendix~\\ref{app:calibration}) suggests temperature scaling is needed for deployment.\n\nBenign-only adaptation addresses human-population distribution shift (WildChat, OASST2) but not cross-LLM distribution shift: adapting on Llama-origin benign conversations reduces FPR but collapses Llama jailbreak detection, as the model conflates LLM-specific style features with benign class membership (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}). Three encoders (125M--355M) differ in architecture and pretraining; same-architecture scaling is needed to isolate the capacity effect. Only persuasion-mediated multi-turn attacks fall within scope; single-turn token-level attacks~\\citep{zou2023gcg}, non-persuasive strategies, and non-text modalities are not addressed. Total pipeline cost is under 4 GPU-hours on a single A100.\n\n\\section*{Ethics Statement}\n\nThis work is defensive: it improves detection of multi-turn jailbreak attacks rather than developing new attacks. We acknowledge dual-use risk, as the persuasion taxonomy could inform adversarial actors. We mitigate this by using only synthetic conversations (no real harmful content) and withholding attack-generation prompts. Detection code, model weights, and evaluation data will be released under CC BY-NC 4.0. No human subjects were involved.\n",

    "originalFile": "% conclusion.tex — D073 Phase 2 revision (page-compressed)\n\n\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Confounds and transferability.}\nThree observations argue against a topic shortcut: unstable topic OOD (F1\\,=\\,0.541$\\pm$0.207), scrambled labels within 3.9\\,pp, and label-free MLM detection (F1\\,$\\geq$\\,0.97). ToxicChat (+18--42\\,pp; Table~\\ref{tab:human_data}) and cross-LLM experiments (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}) confirm transfer; mixed-LLM 9-class maintains DD F1\\,=\\,0.818$\\pm$0.096 while MLM collapses to vanilla level (Appendix~\\ref{app:mixed_llm}). Dataset composition---not sample count---is the primary determinant (Appendix~\\ref{app:scale_validation}). Prompt optimization improves LLM-as-judge OOD detection but does not close the gap with encoder-based methods (Appendix~\\ref{app:prompt_opt}).\n\n\\paragraph{Evidence for discriminative compression.}\nClassification DAPT compresses the feature space into few discriminative dimensions (participation ratio 3.7--9.4 vs.\\ 25--32 for MLM/vanilla) with 3--5 orders of magnitude higher separability (Fisher 2.6--19.7 vs.\\ $<$0.001; Appendix~\\ref{app:representation}). Scrambled labels produce comparable compression (Fisher\\,=\\,2.6), consistent with \\citet{zhang2021understanding}'s framework for how classification modifies representations, with two domain-specific findings: (i)~domain-relevant label partitioning is necessary (topic labels insufficient), and (ii)~compression localizes to 3 SVD dimensions in layers 8--12 (Fisher 15$\\times$ vanilla at peak; Appendix~\\ref{app:layerwise}), with early layers remaining label-independent as in \\citet{maennel2020random}. A discriminative probe confirms: 3 SVD components of 9-class layer-10 representations achieve perfect AA OOD detection (F1\\,=\\,1.000) vs.\\ MLM 0.636 with all 768 dimensions (Appendix~\\ref{app:discriminative_probe}). The complexity-dependent boundary persists ($d < 0.1$ on DD/FITD; $d$\\,=\\,0.44 on AA). Frozen probing near-parity (AA gap 3.1\\,pp) confirms discriminative structure is established in Stage-1.\n\n\\paragraph{Condition-specific advantage.}\nThe evidence hierarchy is specific to multi-turn synthetic evaluation. On ToxicChat (single-turn human data), JB-MLM leads (0.837) while 9-class ranks lower (0.596): 77\\% from false positives and 23\\% from missed attacks lacking multi-turn signals. This reversal is not driven by multi-turn structure: truncating synthetic conversations to a single turn \\emph{widens} the 9-class advantage ($\\bar{\\Delta}$\\,=\\,+1.6\\,pp full $\\to$ +6.0\\,pp single-turn; Appendix~\\ref{app:truncated}), implicating distributional differences between synthetic and human text. Variance reverses (classification std\\,=\\,0.087 vs.\\ MLM\\,=\\,0.013); practitioners facing single-turn human data should consider MLM DAPT.\n\n\\paragraph{Two-axis generalization.}\nOOD F1 reflects attack-side generalization under IID benign conditions. The orthogonal challenge is benign-side shift: DAPT increases WildChat FPR from 48.1\\% to 68.1\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:wildchat_fpr}). Ten target-domain conversations reduce it to 0\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:benign_mixed}); OASST2 confirms the pattern (FPR 57.5\\%\\,$\\rightarrow$\\,1.0\\%$\\pm$0.5\\% at $N$\\,=\\,25; Appendix~\\ref{app:oasst2}).\n\n\\section{Conclusion}\n\nOn synthetic multi-turn data, thirteen encoder configurations decompose jailbreak generalization into attack-side (classification DAPT via discriminative compression; MLM collapses under mixed-LLM data) and benign-side (FPR 65--68\\%\\,$\\rightarrow$\\,0--1.5\\% via 10--25 conversations) axes. Any deterministic partition suffices for template attacks ($d < 0.1$), with a complexity-dependent boundary on ActorAttack ($d$\\,=\\,0.44). At 24.3$\\times$ speedup over LLM-as-judge, these suggest a preliminary two-step protocol.\n\n\\section{Limitations}\n\\label{sec:limitations}\n\nAll multi-turn findings derive from synthetic conversations generated by Qwen3-8B (350 training, 76--118 test per family); preliminary mixed-LLM training (Appendix~\\ref{app:mixed_llm}) suggests this limitation is addressable. Most pairwise comparisons rely on 3 seeds; key scrambled-vs.-correct comparisons are extended to 10 seeds (Appendix~\\ref{app:seed_level}). ToxicChat validates transfer to human-authored single-turn attacks (+18--42\\,pp over vanilla), but the evidence hierarchy reverses on this data (Section~\\ref{sec:discussion}), and no human-generated multi-turn jailbreak dataset exists.\n\nOOD results reflect attack-side generalization only; benign data is IID, so OOD F1 figures are upper bounds. On WildChat, 9-class FPR reaches 68.1\\%; 10 target-domain conversations eliminate it ($n$\\,=\\,49). OASST2 confirms the pattern but requires 25--50 conversations for comparable reduction (FPR\\,=\\,1.0\\%$\\pm$0.5\\% at $N$\\,=\\,25, 3 seeds; Appendix~\\ref{app:oasst2}); validation on additional platforms is needed. Residual calibration error (ECE\\,=\\,0.126--0.141; Appendix~\\ref{app:calibration}) suggests temperature scaling is needed for deployment.\n\nBenign-only adaptation addresses human-population distribution shift (WildChat, OASST2) but not cross-LLM distribution shift: adapting on Llama-origin benign conversations reduces FPR but collapses Llama jailbreak detection, as the model conflates LLM-specific style features with benign class membership (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}). Three encoders (125M--355M) differ in architecture and pretraining; same-architecture scaling is needed to isolate the capacity effect. Only persuasion-mediated multi-turn attacks fall within scope; single-turn token-level attacks~\\citep{zou2023gcg}, non-persuasive strategies, and non-text modalities are not addressed. Total pipeline cost is under 4 GPU-hours on a single A100.\n\n\\section*{Ethics Statement}\n\nThis work is defensive: it improves detection of multi-turn jailbreak attacks rather than developing new attacks. We acknowledge dual-use risk, as the persuasion taxonomy could inform adversarial actors. We mitigate this by using only synthetic conversations (no real harmful content) and withholding attack-generation prompts. Detection code, model weights, and evaluation data will be released under CC BY-NC 4.0. No human subjects were involved.\n",

          "+The evidence hierarchy is specific to multi-turn synthetic evaluation. On ToxicChat (single-turn human data), JB-MLM leads (0.837) while 9-class ranks lower (0.596): 77\\% from false positives. Truncating synthetic conversations to a single turn \\emph{widens} the 9-class advantage ($\\bar{\\Delta}$\\,=\\,+1.6\\,pp $\\to$ +6.0\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:truncated}), implicating distributional differences between synthetic and human text rather than multi-turn structure. Variance reverses (classification std\\,=\\,0.087 vs.\\ MLM\\,=\\,0.013); practitioners facing single-turn human data should consider MLM DAPT.",
          " ",

          " OOD F1 reflects attack-side generalization under IID benign conditions. Ten target-domain conversations reduce it to 0\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:benign_mixed}); OASST2 confirms the pattern (FPR 57.5\\%\\,$\\rightarrow$\\,1.0\\%$\\pm$0.5\\% at $N$\\,=\\,25; Appendix~\\ref{app:oasst2})."
